Dusk fell early under low, sleet-bellied clouds as Sylwia hurried east from Chłodna through the narrow, rubble-choked tenement alleys linking Żelazna to Sienna, cutting five minutes off the Marszałkowska main boulevard so Janek could get two grams of intravenous *Cibazol* before the seven-o'clock ward lockup.

Behind her, nineteen-year-old Tomek closed the gap from twenty paces to six as shadows pooled black inside bombed-out ground-floor storefronts. Six weeks of autumn rain had turned plaster dust into slick grey tallow underfoot; somewhere three blocks south toward the railway cut, a solitary shot cracked—*pop!*—followed by silence. Nobody even turned around for single shots in Warsaw anymore.

*Four to six days,* Sylwia's mind raced like cold clockwork. *Today is Thursday the twenty-eighth. By Monday or Tuesday morning at latest, Otto Reinhardt opens an envelope from Berlin and learns that Sylwia Kotarska and Ludwig Schoeler did not merely inhabit the same city of four million people—they sat three benches apart for three years and graduated tied to the decimal. Once Reinhardt holds that paper, he pulls me straight down into the "tramway" at Aleja Szucha until every bone in my hands is crushed.*

And Marek wanted Schoeler dead by Sunday night.

As she turned into the narrow, crooked brick passage of Sienna Alley—a sixty-yard shortcut between high windowless warehouse walls and a collapsed three-story tannery cratered back in thirty-nine—two men wearing the bottle-green greatcoats and black-visored caps of the *Sonderdienst* auxiliary gendarmerie stepped abruptly out of a recessed coal archway ahead, blocking the narrow brick throat while blowing sour cabbage-and-vodka breath into the sleet.

*Volksdeutsche*—ethnic German recruits from Łódź or the Sudetenland, infinitely louder, greedier, and hair-triggered than regular Wehrmacht soldiers because half of them made triple their pay shaking down black-market smugglers in dark alleys thirty minutes before curfew and shooting witnesses who argued.

Behind Sylwia, Tomek slowed instantly at the alley mouth, lounging against a broken drainpipe as if tying his bootlace, right hand sliding casually inside his frayed jacket lapel. *Don't draw unless you have to, Wróbel,* Sylwia willed silently; a gunfight six blocks from Pavilion Three brought thirty police trucks down on the neighborhood in ten minutes.

"Halt! *Kennkarte* und Tasche auf!" slurred the bigger of the two auxiliaries—six feet of pork-fed Łódź butcher's boy with broken red veins across nose and cheeks, unslinging his Mauser carbine halfway off his shoulder while his leaner, rat-faced companion grinned and stepped to Sylwia's left elbow to box her against the wet brick wall.

Sylwia opened her coat collar just enough to produce her grey Polish *Kennkarte* alongside her red-bordered *Szpital Dzieciątka Jezus* night-surgeon's pass, speaking brisk, haughty Berlin German calculated to make low-ranking *Volksdeutsche* auxiliaries snap their heels together out of reflex:

"Night surgical emergency at Infant Jesus Hospital on Lindleya, *Wachtmeister*, where Oberstleutnant Schoeler of OKH Generalstab is currently quartered under Wehrmacht guard. Inspect the stamp quickly; I am overdue."

Rat-face actually hesitated half a step at *Generalstab*, blinking rapidly in the sleet—until the big red-veined corporal spat brown tobacco juice onto her shoe and snatched her leather doctor's satchel straight out of her hand.

"Generalstab my arse, Sepp—every Polish slut with lipstick and five words of Berlin dialect claims she's wiping some colonel's backside lately! Let's see how many kilos of bacon Frau Doktor's lugging to Lindleya!"

Thick, dirt-rimmed thumbs wrenched the brass frame wide, plunged past the Pelkmann speculum, hooked the loose edge of the oilcloth false divider—and ripped it upward.

In the dim grey sleet-light gleamed four olive-drab cardboard cartons stamped boldly in black Reich ink: **HEERES-SANITÄTSPARK WARSCHAU — UNVERKÄUFLICH WEHRMACHTSEIGENTUM**.

Rat-face sucked in a low, greedy whistle through yellow teeth. "Jesus Maria, Kurt—Wehrmacht sulfa! Five hundred zlotys an ampoule on Kercelak, and four full boxes!"

"Four boxes means firing squad at Pawiak," Kurt grinned slowly, dropping his Mauser back on its sling and shoving Sylwia backward by one shoulder straight into the pitch-dark, roofless brick vestibule of the bombed tannery off the alley so passing pedestrians out on Żelazna wouldn't see them strip the catch. "Keep an eye on the street mouth, Sepp! Let's see what else Frau Doktor's got taped between those long Polish legs before we ring up Aleja Szucha and split the bounty!"

Sylwia's spine hit wet, jagged brick.

Two things happened inside her brain in less than half a heartbeat—not panic, never panic, only that blinding, glacial slowness that descended over an operating table when a major vessel ruptured.

First: if these two lived to blow a whistle, she went to Aleja Szucha tonight with Wehrmacht stolen drugs in her bag—which meant Pawiak torture, Marek's nineteen addresses threatened, Halina arrested, Janek dying downstairs, and her teeth biting down on the eight grains of potassium cyanide sewn three inches above her left collarbone.

Second: Kurt had slung his rifle to use both hands, and her open leather doctor's bag dangled right against his left hip with the unrolled chamois surgical pocket-case hanging halfway out the side.

As Kurt's sour vodka-soaked mouth lunged down toward her throat and his heavy left paw tore the top two horn buttons clean off her wool coat and blouse—cold air and wet rough fingers scraping bare skin above her chemise—Sylwia did not push his chest away. Instead, she let her knees give two inches as though fainting in terror, dropping her right hand level with the dangling chamois instrument roll at his hip, and slid three inches of sterile, razor-honed #11 pointed Parker-Kerr bistoury scalpel cleanly out of its nickel clip.

"Please—" she breathed weakly in broken German, tilting her bare throat leftward so Kurt leaned eagerly into the dark arch after it, leaving the entire left carotid triangle between sternocleidomastoid and trachea stretched taut and uncovered four inches from her chin.

Then she drove the #11 surgical steel straight inward behind the angle of his jaw to the brass bolster, angled thirty degrees medially across common carotid artery, internal jugular vein, and lateral pharyngeal wall, and ripped forward two inches toward the windpipe.

No scream ever left him—only a wet, bubbling *khh-hiss* of severed trachea and hot air.

Scalding scarlet arterial blood erupted across Sylwia's chin, throat, and torn blouse with the force of a fire hydrant, blinding her left eye for half a second as two hundred pounds of dying butcher crashed forward onto her chest, driving her breath out against the brick wall while his boots drummed convulsively in slick plaster mud.

Six paces out at the alley threshold, Rat-face heard the wet thud and spun halfway around from watching the street—*"Kurt? Hat die Katze—"*

From the five-foot rubble pile behind the crumbled archway, nineteen-year-old Tomek launched himself silent as a bobcat onto Rat-face's shoulders, looping eighteen inches of braided steel piano wire rigged between two oak toggles squarely across the auxiliary's throat and wrenching backward with every ounce of wiry Powiśle muscle on his spine.

Rat-face clawed wildly at his buried throat, garbled purple-faced choking muffled to silence as his hobnailed boots kicked frantically against fallen corrugated tin sheeting—*clang! bang-clang!*—loud enough in the twilight alley to carry half a block toward Żelazna!

Shoving Kurt's slackening, spurting carcass sideways off her into the weeds, Sylwia lunged three steps through the dark like an avenging ghost—hair half loose, face painted crimson from cheekbone to collar, #11 blade gleaming black-red in her steady right fist—dropped onto one knee beside the thrashing gendarme's kicking legs, felt once with her left thumb for the femoral pulse below the inguinal crease, no, wool trousers too thick, snapped her hand up to the exposed hollow above Rat-face's straining right collarbone where Tomek's wire stretched the neck taut, and sank the pointed bistoury straight down behind the clavicle through right subclavian artery and trachea together.

*The exact vessel she had sewed shut on Ludwig Schoeler with five-zero silk nine nights ago.*

Three violent jerks, a hot dark gush soaking into green uniform wool, and the hobnailed boots went limp in the mud without striking the tin sheet again.

For ten seconds nobody breathed except the sleet ticking onto rubble and wet nettles. Out on Żelazna fifty yards away, a bicycle bell chimed twice and passed on without stopping.

Tomek unwound the wire from his raw knuckles, chest heaving fast, staring wide-eyed through the gloom at the tall, aristocratic woman surgeon kneeling in black mud beside two dead German policemen, calmly wiping her #11 scalpel clean on the dry inner lining of Kurt's greatcoat before clicking it back into its nickel guard so it wouldn't slice her satchel leather.

"Jesus and Mary, Pani Doktor," Tomek whispered in hushed, terrified awe, crossing himself rapidly before grabbing Rat-face under the boots. "Help me tip them down the flooded dye-vat pit in back before the seven-o'clock patrol comes down Sienna—take his ankles—don't get brains on your skirt hem—"

Working in grim, wordless speed for four minutes, they rolled both bodies—weapons, caps, and all, stuffed down with forty pounds of loose brick—into six feet of black, stagnant cellar water behind collapsed vaulting where rats would leave nothing recognizable before spring thaw, kicked clean wet gravel over the dark puddles in the vestibule, and retrieved all four olive-drab cartons of *Cibazol*.

Behind the ruined cistern stood a wooden rain-barrel filmed with thin evening ice. Cracking the crust with her elbow, Sylwia plunged both bare hands and wrists into freezing water until her bones ached, scrubbing human blood aggressively from beneath every fingernail with a lump of gritty brick-sand, splashing icy water across her cheeks, chin, and throat, drying off with her clean wool headscarf, and pinning her torn blouse and coat lapels high under her chin with two heavy surgical safety pins from her bag so not one speck of scarlet showed on dark charcoal wool.

Only when Tomek melted back into the shadows half a block from Lindleya did Sylwia pause three seconds under the dripping chestnut trees outside Infant Jesus Hospital—waiting patiently until her heart rate slowed from one hundred ten down to seventy-two—before walking cleanly past Hauptmann Brandt's courtyard sentry and Reinhardt's smoking SD plainclothes man in the lobby with a cool, weary nod.

Twenty minutes later, down behind the roaring copper typhus vats in the sub-basement, Halina held the kerosene lantern while Sylwia slid two full five-milliliter ampoules—two grams—of crystal-clear *Cibazol* slowly into delirious young Janek's left cephalic vein, after peeling every trace of German cardboard off and burning the eagle-stamped boxes straight inside the boiler firebox.

At eight-fifteen, washed in sublimate, dressed in a fresh starched white smock, hair re-pinned smooth as polished sable at her nape, Sylwia carried two unwrapped amber glass ampoules of *Cibazol* upstairs to Isolation Room Two.

Hauptmann Brandt was seated by the radiator decoding a Mauerwald flimsy; Ludwig lay propped on two bolsters reading Clausewitz—a fresh pocket edition Brandt had procured—with his good right hand. As Sylwia snapped the scored glass neck off the first unwrapped five-milliliter ampoule inside a gauze square to draw up his evening intravenous dose, a tiny crescent ring of amber glass neck popped sideways out of the gauze fold, bounced silently off her clog sole, and rolled three inches beneath the cast-iron radiator foot without ringing.

Neither Brandt nor Sylwia noticed it.

Approaching the bed with the loaded syringe, Sylwia caught the tourniquet rubber around Ludwig's lean right bicep. As she bent ten inches from his face to slide the bevel smoothly into his median vein, Ludwig lowered his book slowly—slate-blue eyes narrowing just perceptibly as he caught, beneath strong hospital carbolic soap, the faintest trace of freezing rainwater in damp hair behind her ear and a tiny red scratch just below her jaw where Kurt's thumbnail had grazed skin.

He said nothing at all in front of Brandt—only watched her long, steady fingers depress the glass plunger drop by drop, wondering silently whose blood she had washed off them between dusk and eight o'clock.

---

# CHAPTER SEVEN

## The Melian Dialogue

**Warsaw — November 1, 1943**

On the night of All Saints, Warsaw belonged to ghosts.

Standing shirtless in the unlit window embrasure of Isolation Room Two with his field-grey greatcoat draped loose across his braced shoulders against the draft, Ludwig Schoeler parted the edge of the black paper blind by half a finger's breadth and looked down into ulica Lindleya.

Sleet had given way at twilight to a thin, freezing ground-mist smelling of wet dead leaves and coal clinkers. Four hundred meters east toward Marszałkowska, wherever pink execution posters clung peeling to brick walls above scuffed patches of red sand, small points of yellow light bloomed out of nowhere in the gathering dark. Old women in black shawls and ten-year-old girls darted out of tenement archways after the seven-o'clock patrol passed, setting down stub ends of tallow candles sheltered inside cracked jam jars, whispering a five-second *Wieczny odpoczynek*—Eternal rest grant unto them—and melting back into the mist before hobnailed boots rounded the corner again to smash glass and wax into the gutter.

Ten minutes later, out came three more jam jars in the exact same spot.

*Father never understood this city,* Ludwig thought quietly, leaning his good right shoulder against the cold window plaster while a dull, familiar ache throbbed beneath his healing left collarbone. *None of our eastern-march theorists ever did. You can break a Russian army if you encircle its railheads; you can cow Berlin with three police ordinances and a pension cut. Crush Poles under fifty divisions and they turn graveyards into assembly points.*

Twelve days since the ambush on Aleje Jerozolimskie. Yesterday morning Sylwia had pulled the rubber Pezzer tube out from between his sixth and seventh ribs in one smooth, stinging tug on peak expiration and slapped down petrolatum gauze; four days of morning-and-evening intravenous *Cibazol* had dropped his temperature to thirty-seven point one and cleared his pleural effusion clean. He had lost twelve pounds he couldn't easily spare, his left arm still hung strapped across his ribs in a crisp linen sling to keep the ununited collarbone quiet behind those five microscopic threads of black arterial silk, and walking twenty paces left sweat prickling his spine—yet his head was clear as winter crystal.

Behind him, the corridor door opened twice after the regulation knock, and Hauptmann Viktor Brandt stepped inside, shaking sleet from his leather dispatch cape before bolting the latch.

"Well?" Ludwig asked without turning from the tiny candle-sparks down in the mist.

Brandt crossed to the porcelain stove, warming chilled blue fingers against the glazed white tiles while lowering his voice beneath the hiss of steam in the iron standpipe. "Two items, Herr Oberstleutnant. Neither pleasant. First, I took Schnabel out for schnapps behind the Wehrmacht signal exchange at five. Referat Four-A's courier pouch from Berlin Precinct Mitte cleared Poznań at noon; it reaches Aleja Szucha tomorrow afternoon at fourteen hundred hours."

Ludwig let the blackout paper fall shut, cutting off the street candles, and turned into the amber glow of the desk lamp. "Containing?"

"Certified photostats from Reichstagsufer Six—enrollment, class standings, and examination protocols for the graduating *Oberprima* of nineteen-thirty." Brandt hesitated half a beat, his scarred East Prussian face troubled as he met his commander's level gaze. For four years, from the Meuse crossings through Smolensk and Orel, Viktor Brandt had trusted Ludwig Schoeler with his life and—far rarer in nineteen-forty-three—with treasonous truths that would hang both of them from meat hooks in Plötzensee within an hour if whispered to the wrong ear. "You never told me the name of that Polish diplomat's daughter from your Berlin school days, Herr Oberstleutnant. Only old Bredow joked about her once over French brandy outside Vyazma before the mortar got him. *Die Polin*, Bredow called her. Said she was the only human being alive who ever drew blood on you."

Automatically Brandt's glance flicked half an inch toward the thin white crescent above Ludwig's right eyebrow—and then down toward the surgical chart hanging at the foot of the iron bedstead.

"It is the same woman," Brandt said quietly. Not a question.

"Yes, Viktor," Ludwig said.

Brandt exhaled slowly through his nose. "God in heaven. Does Reinhardt suspect?"

"Reinhardt suspects everything that breathes, on principle; tomorrow at two o'clock he stops suspecting and owns paper proof that the woman who saved my life and argued successfully against transferring me to Ujazdów spent three years in the same Berlin classroom with me." Ludwig walked slowly to the oak table, picking up a tiny, curved crescent of snapped amber glass—the scored ampoule neck he himself had spotted gleaming beneath the radiator foot when his pencil rolled onto the linoleum yesterday afternoon. Etched in microscopic white acid numerals around the collar ran: **418-B**. "And your second item?"

Brandt drew a folded carbon flimsy from his inner tunic pocket. "Precinct Four *Ordnungspolizei* fished two missing *Volksdeutsche* auxiliaries out of a flooded tannery cellar off Sienna Alley at noon today. Missing since Thursday evening—six-thirty, half an hour before curfew. No gunshot wounds. One died of a garrote wire plus a single narrow-bladed stab downward behind the right clavicle severing the subclavian artery and trachea; the larger man had his left common carotid and pharynx laid open from behind the jaw angle in one stroke. The police surgeon wrote—I quote—*anatomical precision strongly indicative of professional surgical or slaughterhouse training*."

Silence settled over the warm room like ash.

"Thursday evening at eight-fifteen," Brandt continued very softly, "Frau Doktor Kotarska began your intravenous *Cibazol* course—using unwrapped five-milliliter ampoules from Lot Four-Eighteen-B. The exact batch number reported stolen from Ujazdów supply depot last Tuesday and hawked through Wola black-market channels on Thursday afternoon."

Brandt laid the police flimsy beside the tiny amber glass collar on the oak blotter. He did not reach for a pistol, nor suggest calling the *Feldgendarmerie* outside the door; he simply stood waiting, watching the pale, severe mask of the officer he followed.

"How many copies of this tannery pathology memo reached Aleja Szucha?" Ludwig asked calmly.

"Routine precinct blotter so far—buried among eleven dead railway guards and four shot informers since Thursday," Brandt replied. "Nobody at Precinct Four has connected Sienna Alley to Lot Four-Eighteen-B at Ujazdów, because Ujazdów's quartermaster hushed up the theft to save his own skin from court-martial."

"Good. Burn that carbon in the stove ash-pan before we leave tomorrow morning." Ludwig buttoned the top two buttons of his white linen shirt one-handed—an irritatingly slow exercise he had forced himself to master since yesterday—leaving the lower half open across the broad white adhesive strapping around his ribs. "Have the Mercedes and an armed squad from Wehrmacht Town Major's motor pool—regular *Heer* infantry, Viktor, nobody who answers to SS or Polizei—at the courtyard gate tomorrow morning at zero-eight-hundred. Requisition Order Seven-B confirms Number Twelve Aleja Róż?"

"Confirmed and swept for microphones an hour ago, Herr Oberstleutnant. Empty Danish trade-attaché villa, brick garden wall, three minutes from Ujazdów Park. What about tonight?"

Ludwig glanced at his wristwatch: seven-ten. Five minutes until evening ward rounds.

"Step down to the ground-floor vestibule when Frau Doktor Kotarska comes up, Viktor," Ludwig said evenly. "Keep Reinhardt's two jackals occupied with cigarette rations for twenty minutes, and instruct our corporal outside this door that even if he hears porcelain break, nobody turns that handle unless I call."

Brandt picked up the police carbon, dropped it into the glowing coal slot of the porcelain stove until orange flame licked the grey flimsy to curled black ash, clicked his heels softly, and went out.

Four minutes later, wooden-soled hospital clogs clicked steadily down the second-floor tiles.

Sylwia entered carrying her white enamel dressing tray, nodding briefly to Brandt as the scarred captain passed her on the landing and descended the stairs. Stepping inside Isolation Room Two, she expected to find her patient propped against his bolsters waiting impatiently for his final evening *Cibazol* gram.

Instead, the latch clicked shut behind her—and a warm male hand reached past her shoulder to slide the interior brass bolt home.

Sylwia spun half-around, tray balanced effortlessly against her hip, storm-grey eyes flashing upward as she realized how absurdly different Isolation Room Two felt with Ludwig Schoeler standing upright on his boots.

Even pale, twelve pounds lighter, left forearm cradled cleanly in white linen across his chest and field-grey greatcoat slung dark over his shoulders, six feet two inches of upright Prussian staff officer swallowed half the narrow whitewashed room. Four hours earlier, down in the laundry sluice room, Halina had pressed a tiny rolled tissue spill from Marek into Sylwia's wet palm before burning it: *Brandt moves him tomorrow morning. Our watchers confirm Aleja Róż. No second chance inside German district. Photograph briefcase tonight or finish the vein before midnight. For Zbyszek. — G.*

On her white enamel tray, beside the loaded five-milliliter Pravaz syringe of clear *Cibazol*, rested a clean, empty twenty-cubic-centimeter glass Luer syringe—ostensibly for aspirating any recurrent pleural fluid. Twenty cubic centimeters of room air driven briskly into an antecubital vein foamed right-ventricular blood into compressible froth inside twelve heartbeats: acute pulmonary air lock, indistinguishable at clinical bedside from massive saddle thromboembolism out of a pelvic vein after twelve days' bedrest.

Her fingers had been icy since four o'clock. Now, looking up into Ludwig's clear, perilous slate-blue eyes across four feet of yellow lamplight, every survival bell in her skull clanged at once.

"Get back on that mattress, Herr Oberstleutnant," Sylwia said sharply, setting the enamel tray down on the oak table with a crisp *clink*. "Standing twenty minutes on an unhealed clavicle pulls the lateral trapezius upward and stresses—"

Her voice stopped.

Resting squarely in the center of the oak blotter under the gooseneck lamp, six inches from where she had just set her tray, lay a tiny snapped collar of amber glass etched in white acid: **418-B**.

Sylwia did not gasp, did not look down twice, did not touch her pocket. Only her jaw set one degree harder as she raised her grey eyes straight into his.

"Careless housekeeping on Thursday night, Kotarska," Ludwig murmured conversationally, leaning his lean hip slightly against the edge of the oak table while his unbound right hand rested inches from her tray. "Half of Lot Four-Eighteen-B went missing from Ujazdów Reserve-Lazarett last week. Two *Volksdeutsche* gendarmes who stopped a tall, Berlin-spoken Polish woman doctor in Sienna Alley at six-thirty Thursday evening turned up in a tannery cistern today—one with his carotid opened from behind the mandible, the other stabbed cleanly downward behind the right collarbone into the subclavian trunk."

His gaze dropped momentarily to the twenty-cubic-centimeter empty glass syringe glinting on her sterile towel, then rose calmly back to her face.

"Remarkable symmetry, wouldn't you say? Five days after sewing a left subclavian shut in the dark, somebody opens a right one in pitch-black mud without missing by two millimeters. Tell me—was the twenty-milliliter syringe on your tray tonight meant to tap my chest, Sylwia, or blow twenty cubic centimeters of Warsaw air into my right ventricle?"

Silence stretched so tight between them the hiss of the steam pipe sounded like escaping gas.

Any denial was beneath both of them; fifteen years ago in Berlin they had abandoned lying to each other once caught red-handed because lies wasted valuable seconds better spent counter-attacking.

"Would anyone in heaven or earth blame me if I did?" Sylwia asked very softly, standing straight as a surgical probe two paces in front of him, hands relaxed at her sides where three inches separated her fingers from the scalpel forceps on the tray. "Those two animals in Sienna Alley ripped my blouse open to rape me before handing me over to Aleja Szucha for carrying twenty ampoules of sulfa—ten of which saved *your* rotten Prussian pleura from empyema since Thursday! And my people outside know why Mauerwald sent you here, Ludwig. They know about *Kronos*."

Not a muscle flickered around Ludwig's eyes at the code word—which told Sylwia instantly that Marek's London intercept was dead accurate.

"Your people outside," Ludwig replied, voice dropping to cold, lethal velvet, "know precisely what Otto Reinhardt spoon-fed them! Use that brain Doktor Glass spent three years polishing, Sylwia! Who tipped your Wola gunmen off that a senior staff car switched onto the Małkinia loop on October nineteenth? Who wants *Kronos* kept running on a dirty string long enough to trace London's transmitters while blaming Wehrmacht intelligence for leaks? And what do you imagine happens to *you* at two-fifteen tomorrow afternoon if I die in this bed tonight?"

"I already know what arrives at Aleja Szucha tomorrow afternoon," Sylwia shot back coolly, enjoying the fractional narrowing of his slate eyes as she matched his intelligence network card for card. "Photostats from Precinct Mitte. Reichstagsufer Six, nineteen-twenty-seven through nineteen-thirty. Ignacy Słowik's bird on Szucha sang three days ago."

"Magnificent," Ludwig breathed, genuine, fierce admiration flashing across his lean face despite the abyss yawning directly beneath both their boots. "God in heaven, only you would have a spy inside Reinhardt's own teleprinter room while dodging hangmen in Wola! Then finish the equation, *Frau Doktor*! Suppose you put twenty cubic centimeters of air into my arm right now—go ahead, I have one arm strapped to my ribs and twelve pounds gone, you might even manage it before I break your wrist. At eight tomorrow morning I am found dead of 'embolism.' Six hours later, Otto Reinhardt opens the Berlin envelope, discovers Ludwig Schoeler's deadliest childhood companion held the syringe all week, drags you straight down into the cellars of Aleja Szucha before curfew, finds those wounded boys groaning behind your typhus boilers downstairs, and hangs every doctor and nurse in Pavilion Three!"

Every word struck like cold iron hammer-blows because every single word was mathematically irrefutable. She had been turning that exact nightmare cube over in her skull since Chłodna.

"Whereas if you walk out of here alive tomorrow morning to Number Twelve Aleja Róż," Sylwia countered bitterly, "Reinhardt *still* opens that Berlin envelope at two o'clock and arrests me by three!"

"No, he doesn't," Ludwig said. Reaching inside his open white shirt pocket with his right hand, he drew out a stiff cream card stamped in crimson Generalstab eagle-and-swastika wax over Generaloberst Zeitzler's counter-intelligence seal and tossed it beside the amber glass ring.

Sylwia glanced down without touching it.

**OBERKOMMANDO DES HEERES — ABWEHRSTELLE / SONDERAUSWEIS NR. 114.** By authority of OKH Special Investigation Staff, **Dr. med. Sylwia Kotarska** of *Szpital Dzieciątka Jezus* was hereby commandeered under exclusive Wehrmacht military jurisdiction as attending thoracic surgeon to Oberstleutnant i.G. Ludwig Schoeler at Aleja Róż Twelve, immune from civil or *Sicherheitspolizei* detention save upon counter-signature of Army Group Centre Counter-Espionage.

Sylwia stared at the crimson seal as if it were a live cobra coiled on oak. "Are you out of your mind? Slapping an OKH protection warrant on my back the exact day Reinhardt learns we knew each other in Berlin? That practically screams conspiracy in his ear!"

"Let it scream," Ludwig said icily. "Reinhardt is an ex-Munich homicide detective, not a berserker like Kutschera. When he sees an open, brazen Generalstab requisition signed twenty-four hours *before* his Berlin mail arrives, he pauses—because arresting an OKH-commandeered surgeon without hard treason proof starts a jurisdictional war with Mauerwald, AND he instantly assumes I am either blackmailing an old Berlin acquaintance into spying on Warsaw doctors or sleeping with her! Either theory keeps you out of Pawiak's basement while his hounds shadow us—provided we find his mole *Jaskółka* first."

*Jaskółka.* Second time he had spoken of a double-agent; first time he had named it.

"You expect me," Sylwia whispered, incredulous fury rising hot in her chest, "to walk through the German quarter every evening into your villa under Marek's binoculars—looking to the entire Polish Underground like a Gestapo colonel's kept whore—to help *Abwehr* hunt a spy?"

"Not my spy—*Reinhardt's* spy inside your own house!" Ludwig snapped back, stepping one long pace around the corner of the oak table until barely eighteen inches separated them. "Somebody inside or touching this hospital tipped off my train *and* told Reinhardt within forty-eight hours that you bolted this door on Friday night! Until *Jaskółka* stops singing, neither your cellar boys nor my people leave Warsaw alive!"

"*Your* people?" she challenged sharply. "Wehrmacht intelligence come to crush *Kronos*?"

"Wait until we're behind stone walls I swept myself before you presume to know why I came to Warsaw, Kotarska!"

"I won't wear a German leash—not Reinhardt's and never yours!" And before her cooler brain could check the fierce, lifelong reflex never to leave evidence sitting under Ludwig Schoeler's hand, Sylwia darted her right fingers sideways across the blotter to snatch the `418-B` amber glass ring and crush it under her clog heel.

Even one-armed, Ludwig moved faster than eyesight.

His right hand shot down, intercepting her left wrist—no, her right wrist inches above the glass—and as she twisted violently leftward bringing her free hand toward the blunt bandage shears to force him back, Ludwig stepped squarely inside her swing, caught her left forearm against her own ribs by pinning her backward with the hard wall of his braced chest and right shoulder, and backed her two swift steps flat against the tall, hot glazed-porcelain tiles of the corner stove.

"Don't—" Sylwia gasped, breath knocked half out of her as smooth hot porcelain met her shoulder blades through thin starched cotton and six feet two inches of hard, lean muscle locked her immovable from hip to collarbone.

His unbound right hand slid rapidly up to trap both her crossed wrists firmly against the upper swell of her own chest just below her throat—careful even in fury not to twist radius bones, utterly relentless in holding her fast—while his right thigh wedged squarely between her knees, spreading her stance three inches on the linoleum so she couldn't drive a knee into his groin as she had boasted sixteen years ago on the Marschallbrücke.

Instantly, from the sudden violent exertion of pinning a tall fighting woman one-handed, fresh hot arterial-bright blood seeped from his Friday-night fourth-rib incision through the white strapping and bloomed in a warm crimson coin the size of a silver mark directly through his unfastened white linen shirt—pressing wet and hot right against the thin cotton covering her left breast.

Both of them felt the wet heat strike through cloth at the exact same second.

Both of them froze.

Thirty-two years old, stove heat baking into Sylwia's spine, sleet ticking against blackout paper three yards away, and suddenly sixteen years vanished into thin smoke. Up this close, Ludwig was no phantom from Berlin classrooms nor distant uniform across a ward; he was overwhelming, suffocatingly real—broad shoulders blocking out the room, fever-pared jaw dark with evening golden stubble two inches above her forehead, chest rising and falling hard and ragged against her breasts with every breath, smelling of clean skin, hot linen, iron blood, and winter wool.

"Let go of me, Ludwig," she whispered—except something dark and treacherous down in her own lungs robbed the command of half its ice, turning her voice into a low, uneven scrape that made his slate-blue eyes darken almost to black.

"So you can stab me with Lister shears or blow air into my heart?" he murmured back, breath hot across her cheekbone and parted lips, neither moving back an inch nor loosening his grip on her crossed wrists. "Look at me and tell me you didn't feel that blood soak through onto your skin just now, Sylwia. My blood—or yours, God knows which half is whose anymore."

"Get off me before your subclavian tears—"

"Let it tear," he breathed harshly, head dipping an inch lower until the thin white crescent scar above his right eyebrow brushed a loose dark tendril at her temple, his thumb shifting half an inch upward off her wrists to rest against the frantic, traitorous hammering of her carotid pulse just beneath her jaw.

And then his calloused thumb slid one centimeter lower along the starched seam of her high smock collar—and struck the hard, unmistakable cherry-stone cylinder of paraffined glass sewn inside the linen.

Ludwig went stone-still from head to heel.

Every staff officer who had ever interrogated captured British SOE agents or Soviet parachutists knew the exact dimensions and feel of an eight-grain potassium cyanide ampoule stitched behind a collar lapel.

Slowly, terribly, Ludwig lifted his eyes from the white linen seam at her collarbone straight into her wide storm-grey eyes. What flared in his face now wasn't tactical calculation, nor schoolboy rivalry, nor even anger—it was a raw, ferocious blaze of sheer possessive fury that shook Sylwia harder than the scalpel fight in Sienna Alley.

Pressing his thumb squarely between the cyanide bead and her jaw so she felt the hard outline against her own skin, Ludwig lowered his mouth until his lips barely brushed the corner of hers in a ghost-touch hotter than open flame.

"Listen to me, Sylwia Kotarska," he whispered straight against her mouth, every syllable vibrating down through her ribs into the pit of her stomach. "You will walk through the gate of Twelve Aleja Róż at eighteen hundred hours tomorrow carrying that black bag of yours. We will find *Jaskółka* before Reinhardt touches a hair of your head. And if you ever put your teeth near this glass bead while my heart is still beating in this city, I swear before God I will break every door down between Mauerwald and Pawiak and pry your jaws open with my bare hands."

For two endless, dizzying seconds neither of them breathed—her lips parted an eighth of an inch beneath the heat of his, her pulse thundering wildly under his thumb, fifteen years of hatred twisting into an ache so sharp and dark she didn't know whether she wanted to sink her teeth into his lower lip or pull his head down with both hands.

Outside in the corridor, heavy boots shifted once beside the frosted glass as Brandt returned up the stairs and cleared his throat loudly before knocking.

Ludwig released her wrists cleanly, stepped back two paces to the oak table, picked up the `418-B` amber glass collar, dropped it under the iron heel of his riding boot, and ground it into unrecognizable powder on the floor.

"Eighteen hundred hours tomorrow, Frau Doktor," he said in crisp, impersonal Generalstab German just as Brandt rapped twice on the frame. "Leave the *Cibazol*; we are finished here for tonight."

---

# CHAPTER EIGHT

## Abitur

**Berlin — May to June 1930**

By the spring of nineteen-thirty, the champagne in Berlin had gone sour at the bottom of the glass, though the bands played twice as loud to drown out the marching boots.

Every morning as Counselor Kotarski's Mercedes carried Sylwia eastward from Kurfürstenstraße toward the Spree, pasted election bills peeled off damp hoardings in red and black shrieks—swastikas over Wedding, hammer-and-sickle fists over Neukölln, six million pairs of idle hands shoved into frayed pockets outside shuttered machine works after the New York crash. Even inside the tall, tranquil brick walls of Reichstagsufer Six, shadows lengthened: Ruth Oppenheim's father quietly transferred half his banking house to Amsterdam in April, and at supper on May tenth Kazimierz Kotarski laid down his silver fish-knife and told Sylwia that Foreign Minister Zaleski had recalled him to Warsaw effective June twenty-second.

"Germany has caught typhus of the soul, *córeczko*," her father said quietly, watching twilight turn the chestnut blossoms purple outside the tall windows. "Stresemann is dead, Brüning rules by presidential decree, and inside three years either the generals or the beer-hall screamers will own the Wilhelmstraße. You will sit your *Abitur* next month so no Prussian ever claims you left unfinished, and in October you will enter the medical faculty at the University of Warsaw."

Sylwia nodded calmly, buttered a slice of rye bread, went upstairs to her bedroom, shut the oak door, and stood staring out toward the Tiergarten for twenty minutes while an inexplicable hollow ache—sharp as hunger, humiliating as cowardice—clawed behind her ribs.

She told herself she was furious merely because three weeks left barely enough time to beat Ludwig Schoeler cleanly in the Prussian State *Abitur*.

At eighteen—she would turn nineteen in September, Ludwig nineteen in July—their legendary three-year war had drawn spectators from across the Berlin school board. Bets ran three-to-one in the masters' common room on a dead heat, and when the week-long written papers concluded on June twelfth, Privy Councilor Dr. Hagemann of the Provincial School Collegium took the unprecedented step of presiding personally over the final oral examinations in the Great Aula.

All morning under the tall portraits of Frederick the Great and Jean Calvin, Ludwig and Sylwia fenced across four disciplines like two young maîtres d'armes who had forgotten the rest of the room existed.

When Hagemann tested Latin sight rhetoric from Tacitus's *Agricola*—*Ubi solitudinem faciunt, pacem appellant*, where they make a desert, they call it peace—Ludwig delivered an icy, architectural analysis of Roman frontier pacification that had two Reichswehr observers nodding approval; Sylwia followed immediately by dissecting Tacitus's syntax as an indictment of imperial self-delusion so razor-sharp that Herr Doktor Glass hid a grin behind three fingers. In organic chemistry they tied without a slip; in differential calculus Ludwig finished a surface-integral proof on the slate four seconds ahead of Sylwia, only for Sylwia to tap his third line with chalk on her way past and point out a dropped negative sign he corrected half a heartbeat before Hagemann turned his spectacles.

At five o'clock, after forty minutes behind locked oak doors while sixty graduating seniors sweated in stiff collars and white blouses down in the courtyard, Oberstudiendirektor Becker emerged onto the stone steps and read the cumulative three-year *Reifeprüfung* averages aloud.

*Schoeler, Ludwig Heinrich: Eins Komma Null Null.*
*Kotarska, Sylwia Maria: Eins Komma Null Null.*

An audible gasp ran across the courtyard. Never since the *Französisches Gymnasium* admitted female pupils had two candidates tied to the hundredth decimal at absolute perfection.

"Inasmuch," Becker continued stiffly, clearing his throat over the cream parchment while avoiding Doktor Glass's sardonic single eye, "as the statutes of the Humboldt Foundation provide for a single recipient of the silver *Primus Omnium* medallion and valedictory chair, the Collegium awards precedence to Senior Matriculant Ludwig Schoeler in recognition of continuous enrollment since *Sexta* and prospective service to the State."

Polite, scattered applause rose. Ruth Oppenheim grabbed Sylwia's cold wrist indignantly—*"It's highway robbery because you're a girl and Polish!"*—while ten yards away under the linden tree, tall in dark blue worsted, Ludwig stood holding the flat blue-velvet medallion box without opening it, looking straight across thirty bobbing heads into Sylwia's storm-grey eyes.

He was not smiling. For the first time in three years, winning tasted manifestly of ashes to him too.

"Pack your evening trunk last," Sylwia told her maid briskly on the afternoon of Saturday, June twenty-first—Midsummer Eve, ten hours before the seven-forty Danzig-Warsaw express pulled out of Friedrichstraße Station. "Leave out the red Paris crepe."

Kazimierz raised one silver brow over his strapped leather dispatch boxes in the stripped, echoing drawing room. "You still intend going out to Wannsee tonight? Generalmajor Schoeler's invitation was strictly pro forma to the graduating class—and his nephew Horst struts around Tiergarten in brown breeches now."

"If I stay home tonight, Papa," Sylwia said serenely, fastening small pearl drops at her ears before the bare mantel mirror, "Ludwig Schoeler will tell himself for the next fifty years that I hid under my bed crying over a piece of Prussian silver."

At nine-thirty that hot, oppressive June evening, air hung motionless and sultry as warm velvet above the black mirror of the Wannsee. Far to the west beyond Potsdam, silent copper lightning pulsed inside towering anvil clouds without thunder yet. Across the wide terraced lawns of the Schoeler villa on Große Seestraße, strings of glowing paper lanterns swayed faintly above white-draped champagne tables while a five-piece Berlin dance band played *Ich bin von Kopf bis Fuß auf Liebe eingestellt*—Marlene Dietrich's languid, shameless new song from *Der blaue Engel*—sending muted trumpet notes drifting out across the reeds and moored racing yachts.

When Sylwia descended the stone garden steps alone—her father had wisely pleaded farewell calls at the French Embassy and sent his chauffeur to wait outside the iron gates at midnight—conversation rippled and stalled across three tables.

She had left behind every trace of schoolgirl navy wool. The Parisian bias-cut gown of deep arterial-crimson silk crepe clung like poured wine over tall, long-limbed curves nineteen years had suddenly ripened, cut daringly low in a clean V down her bare back to the small hollow above her waist, dark chestnut hair brushed out of braids for once into heavy, shining waves pinned back behind one ear with an antique silver Mazovian leaf-clasp of her mother's.

Standing near the balustrade flanked by two blond *Herrenreiter* cousins and a plump girl in pink tulle hanging onto his sleeve, Ludwig stopped dead in the middle of lifting a champagne flute.

In white summer mess-style evening waistcoat and black tails, sun-bronzed along his lean cheekbones from June sculling on the lake, tarnished-gold hair gleaming under the terrace lamps and that thin white crescent scar pale above his right brow, he looked so arrestingly, furiously handsome that Sylwia's stomach dropped two inches before pride locked her spine straight.

For forty minutes neither approached the other. Sylwia drank one glass of dry Moselle, danced a brisk foxtrot with loyal Kurt von Bredow—who blushed crimson down to his collar stud holding her bare waist—accepted Doktor Glass's dry farewell blessing by the punch bowl, and waited until ten-fifteen when the first fat, warm drops of thunderstorm rain hissed against the paper lanterns, driving forty laughing guests indoors toward the ballroom buffet.

Slipping unseen down the hedged gravel path away from the house instead of up the steps, Sylwia walked swift and barefoot—carrying her crimson satin slippers in one hand so the heels wouldn't sink into wet turf—straight down onto the long cedar pier jutting into the dark lake.

Ten seconds later, rapid footsteps crunched down the gravel behind her.

She didn't need to turn around to know whose stride measured thirty-four inches. Thunder rolled low and heavy across the dark water as warm rain abruptly thickened from scattered drops into a pounding silver sheet, plastering crimson silk instantly against her hips and cooling her bare shoulders.

"Have you lost whatever wits Becker left you, Kotarska?" Ludwig called sharply through the roar of rain on lake water, catching her bare upper arm firmly at the pier head and pulling her sideways through the side door of the vaulted cedar boathouse before slamming the oak latch against the squall.

Inside, pitch darkness smelled intensely of cedar shavings, spar varnish, linseed oil, and wet lake weeds lapping softly inside the open central water-slip where his sleek single racing scull rocked gently against rope fenders. Above the high transom window, blue-white lightning flashed every ten seconds across the Wannsee, turning rain-streaked glass into strobes of silver fire.

Sylwia shook his hand off her wet arm, tossed her dry satin slippers onto the carpenter's bench beside a stack of canvas boat-cushions, and faced him across four feet of shadowed planking while rainwater ran warm from her dark hair down her collarbones.

"Seven-forty tomorrow morning from Friedrichstraße," she said without preamble, chest rising quick and shallow in the thunder-charged air. "I came to collect half that medal with a hacksaw before I shake Berlin dust off my shoes forever."

Ludwig didn't laugh. Standing tall in rain-spotted black wool tails, water gleaming on his golden lashes and high cheekbones when lightning flared through the transom, he reached slowly inside his white waistcoat pocket, drew out the flat blue-velvet box, snapped it open, and tossed the heavy two-ounce silver Humboldt medallion straight into her outstretched palm.

"Take the whole thing," he said quietly.

Sylwia stared down at the cold silver disc in her wet hand—engraved *PRIMUS OMNIUM — COLLÈGE FRANÇAIS BERLIN 1930*—and felt rage ten times hotter than defeat blaze behind her eyes.

"I don't take charity from Schoelers!"

"It isn't charity, damn your stubborn Polish skull!" Ludwig snapped, taking one sharp step closer across the cedar boards. "Half the school knows Becker rigged the statute on my father's telephone call so a foreign girl wouldn't read the Latin address from Frederick's podium! Do you think I want a rigged piece of tin sitting on my desk when you beat me on Tacitus in front of Glass?"

"You beat me by four seconds on the surface integral!"

"With a dropped minus sign you handed back to me instead of letting me hang in front of Hagemann!" His voice roughened suddenly, losing every trace of cool Dahlem polish under the drumming roar of rain on cedar shingles overhead. "Why the devil did you tap that chalkboard line, Sylwia? Three years you've tried to cut my throat over quarter-marks, and with the whole Prussian board watching you saved my proof!"

"Because if I beat you," she cried fiercely, throat closing hard around something unbearable she refused to name, "I beat you at full strength, never on a slip of chalk! Keep your wretched medal—choke on it at Königsberg or Potsdam or wherever your father marches you!"

Spinning half around toward the open rectangular water-slip in the middle of the boathouse floor, Sylwia drew back her arm and hurled the heavy silver medallion straight out through the open lake-gate—*plop!*—into six feet of black, rain-lashed Wannsee water.

"Are you completely mad?!" Ludwig lunged forward, catching both her bare wet shoulders from behind and spinning her squarely back around to face him at the lip of the dark slip.

"Yes!" she gasped, shoving both palms hard against his rain-damp white evening shirtfront—except instead of pushing him away, her fingers curled spasmodically into fine linen lawn over the violent, hammering thunder of his heart. "Three years in this damned arrogant city, and after seven-forty tomorrow morning who am I supposed to fight with among polite Warsaw fools who smile when I talk?!"

Lightning split the clouds directly over Peacock Island with a simultaneous, deafening *CRACK* of thunder that shook the cedar rafters—illuminating Ludwig's face inches above hers, stripped raw of every mask he had worn since sixteen.

"Nobody," Ludwig breathed harshly—and crushed his mouth down onto hers.

It was not gentle; gentleness would have been a lie after a thousand days of war.

His mouth took hers hot, starved, bruisingly hard, tasting of dry Moselle, rainwater, and salt, and before half a heartbeat passed Sylwia made a low, broken sound deep in her throat, flung both bare arms up around his neck sending her mother's silver hair-clasp clattering forgotten onto the cedar planks, and kissed him back with teeth and tongue and three years of dammed-up wildfire.

"Sylwia—Christ in heaven, Sylwia—" he groaned into her open mouth, lifting her clean off her bare feet by her waist and backing blindly away from the black water-slip until the back of her thighs struck the sturdy waist-high carpenter's bench piled thick with canvas yacht cushions. Setting her down onto the cushions so her eyes came level with his, Ludwig buried one trembling hand deep in her heavy wet chestnut hair while his other slid down her bare spine inside the deep V of crimson silk, pressing her arched body hard against his chest.

Every nerve in Sylwia's nineteen-year-old body went up like dry pine soaked in ether. Never—not once in Berlin or Warsaw—had she let any boy put more than polite gloved fingers on her waist at a dance; she had despised their soft hands and vapid compliments because none of them were *him*. Now her own fingers tore frantically at his pearl collar stud until it popped bouncing across the dark floorboards, ripping his stiff white wing-collar open so she could press her open mouth and teeth straight against the scorching, salt-slick pulse hammering at the base of his throat.

Ludwig shuddered violently from shoulders to boots. Catching the thin crimson silk straps at her shoulders, he dragged both downward past her collarbones; bias-cut Parisian crepe slipped whispering to her waist in the blue flash of the next lightning bolt, baring high, pale, rose-tipped breasts that had never known cool night air or a man's eyes, nipples tightening pebble-hard instantly before his hot palm cupped her left breast and his mouth dropped down to take the right cleanly between lips and tongue.

Sylwia's head fell back against the stacked canvas cushions with a sharp, shameless cry muffled only by thunder overhead, bare heels locking instinctively behind his lean thighs in black evening wool to pull his hips tight into the open cradle of hers. Through thin silk crepe rucked halfway up her thighs and damp French lace beneath, the thick, straining iron ridge behind his trouser fly ground hard directly against the aching center between her legs—sending such a shocking, liquid jolt of pure carnal heat through her belly that her fingernails dug straight through his linen shirt into the muscles of his shoulders.

"Look at me," Ludwig rasped against her wet breastbone, lifting his flushed, wild face in the shadowy blue dark while his right hand bunched crimson silk ruthlessly up to her hips, calloused rower's fingers sliding up the bare warm satin of her inner thigh past silk garter ribbons, hooking aside the narrow damp gusset of her lace knickers, and touching bare, slick, burning flesh for the first time.

Sylwia gasped his name aloud—*"Ludwig—oh God, Ludwig—"*—hips bucking involuntarily into his palm as two long fingers parted dripping folds, circling once over the swollen, hypersensitive bud at the apex until white sparks burst behind her eyelids before sliding one finger, then two, deep inside the tight, untried heat of her body.

"God help me, you're shaking—" he whispered feverishly, forehead pressed hard against hers, breathing raggedly into her open mouth while his thumb stroked slow, devastating circles over slick nerves in rhythm with the deep curl of his fingers inside her, watching her grey eyes go blind and black-pupiled as the first violent, shattering wave of pleasure in her life clamped her inner muscles tight as a fist around his hand. Before the last shudder stopped quivering down her thighs, his free hand tore open the black horn buttons at his fly, freeing himself hot and heavy between their tangled bodies, broad tip nudging slick and urgent right at the threshold of her entrance while she gripped his bare neck with both hands, whispering fiercely against his bruised lips: *"Don't talk—now, Ludwig, before tomorrow comes, I don't care if it hurts—"*

"Don't get on that train tomorrow," he breathed against her throat, half out of his mind, kissing her jaw, her eyelids, her mouth while easing half an inch forward against tight resistance so she wouldn't tear—"Tell your father Paris, Zurich, Heidelberg, I don't care where, I'll walk out of Dahlem tonight without one pfennig of my father's money if I have to—I can't wake up in this damned city knowing you aren't—"

Up on the terraced path forty yards above the boathouse, gravel crunched heavily under four pairs of boots, and a harsh, drunken baritone sliced straight through the slackening rain:

*"Look on the pier railing, Rudolf—silver silk wrap! Told you Counselor Kotarski's stuck-up little Warsaw filly slipped down toward the water!"*

Both of them froze dead—hearts slamming like trapped birds against bare skin, Ludwig buried half an inch at the threshold of her body, sweat turning to ice water inside a single second.

Through the narrow ventilation gap between cedar wall-boards ten feet away, yellow storm-lantern beams swung wildly down the gravel steps toward the pier head. Sylwia caught one glimpse of brown twill breeches, polished black jackboots, and a red-white-black swastika armband plastered damply around a thick bicep—Horst Schoeler, Ludwig's twenty-four-year-old cousin from the Charlottenburg *Sturmabteilung*, flanked by two burly older men in civilian trench coats wearing police reserve pins, while thirty paces higher up the slope Generalmajor Heinrich Schoeler's tall dark silhouette stood watching under an umbrella.

"Keep your voice down, Horst, the old man's on the path," muttered one of the trench coats right outside the pier planks. "If she's necking with young Bredow or one of the French boys in the boathouse, haul them both down to Precinct Twelve until ten tomorrow morning for public indecency—old Kotarski misses the seven-forty diplomatic courier express looking for his brat, and Abteilung IA gets twelve clean hours to search his unsealed baggage at Friedrichstraße for those Pomeranian artillery transcripts!"

Inside the dark cedar boathouse, Sylwia couldn't understand *Pomeranian transcripts*, only *Precinct Twelve* and *search her father's baggage*—and felt every muscle across Ludwig's bare chest and shoulders turn slowly from living flesh into six inches of solid, arctic Krupp armor.

Looking down at her through the gloom—crimson silk bunched around her waist, bare breasts still glistening from his mouth, grey eyes wide and trusting—Ludwig closed his eyes once, so tightly a muscle spasmed beside the scar over his brow.

*Three armed men outside, my father thirty yards behind them with friends at Police Headquarters, and if they drag her into Alexanderplatz tonight they get Kotarski's papers and break both of them.*

Carefully, silently, Ludwig withdrew from her body, fastened his trousers in two swift jerks, and pulled the crimson silk straps gently back up over her bare shoulders—smoothing the bias-cut crepe down past her knees before picking up her dry satin slippers from the bench and pressing them into her numb hands.

"Put your shoes on," he whispered so low the lapping water almost drowned it. "Whatever I say out on that dock in the next sixty seconds—keep your chin up, walk straight to your father's car at the gate, and do not say one word back. Swear it."

Confused, still dizzy with fading heat and sudden cold dread, she slipped her heels into satin. "Ludwig, what—"

Outside, Horst Schoeler's fist hammered thrice against the oak boathouse door. *"Hallo inside! Anybody drowning in there, or shall we break the latch?!"*

Ludwig deliberately raked one hand rough through his damp golden hair, left his white evening collar unbuttoned and dangling loose with a faint smear of Sylwia's carmine lip-salve visible near his throat, unbolted the oak door, and strolled out onto the rain-slick pier beneath the swinging storm-lanterns wearing a lazy, bored, insufferably aristocratic sneer.

"Lower that lantern out of my eyes, Horst, before I kick it into the Wannsee," Ludwig drawled coolly, fastening one waistcoat button at his leisure while blocking the narrow doorway so neither police crony could peer inside at rumpled boat cushions. "Can't a man finish saying goodbye to his graduating class without half the Charlottenburg beer-hall brigade tramping down the pier?"

Horst grinned nastily as Sylwia stepped out into the drizzling lantern light behind Ludwig's shoulder, crimson gown damp at the hem, dark hair tumbled loose over her bare shoulders without its silver leaf-clasp. Both trench-coated men stiffened greedily forward—*"Fräulein Kotarska of the Polish Legation, isn't it? Rather irregular hour—"*

"Save your breath, gentlemen," Ludwig interrupted with a short, contemptuous laugh that froze every drop of blood in Sylwia's veins solid to her fingertips. Reaching into his waistcoat pocket, he flicked a bright silver ten-Reichsmark piece lazily through the wet air; it rang *tink-spin* onto the wet cedar planks two inches from Sylwia's right satin toe. "Nothing left here worth a precinct blotter. Bredow bet me ten marks in *Obersekunda* that Warsaw ice wouldn't thaw before graduation night. Three years was tedious work, I grant you—Polish mares always kick hardest before they take the bit—but a Schoeler never leaves an open ledger in Berlin."

Horst stared two seconds from the ten-mark coin at Sylwia's feet to the lipstick smudge on Ludwig's open throat, and burst into a coarse, roaring bark of laughter, slapping his trench-coated police friend across the shoulder. "Ten marks! Christ, Ludwig, the old general worried you had monk's blood in your veins, and here you're settling Versailles reparations on your back! Leave her be, Rudolf—damaged goods won't stop old Kotarski's train now anyway, she'll cry into her pillow all the way to Posen!"

Twenty feet up the terraced steps under the umbrella, Generalmajor Heinrich Schoeler looked down once, nodded with faint, wintry satisfaction, and turned back toward the lighted villa.

On the wet cedar pier, Sylwia Kotarska stood motionless in crimson silk.

She did not tremble. She did not cry out. She did not slap him—because slapping him would prove to these grinning jackals that Ludwig Schoeler still possessed the power to hurt living nerves inside her body, and within those ten seconds everything nineteen years old and vulnerable inside Sylwia Kotarska burned cleanly down to white, sterile bone ash.

Looking straight through Ludwig Schoeler's pale, expressionless face as though empty lake water stood behind his waistcoat, she stepped neatly past the gleaming ten-Reichsmark coin without touching it with her hem, picked up her dropped silver shawl from the wet railing, and walked up the long gravel drive alone through the rain to her father's waiting car.

She never looked back at Friedrichstraße Station at seven-forty the next morning.

Therefore she never knew that at two o'clock in the morning, after the last car left Große Seestraße, nineteen-year-old Ludwig Schoeler knelt vomiting champagne and blood-tinged bile into the black reeds behind the boathouse; stripped naked in thunder-chill rain to dive seven times into black silt until his raw, bleeding fingers closed around both the silver Humboldt medallion and her mother's fallen Mazovian silver leaf-clasp on the boathouse floor; and at half past two walked into the stable courtyard where Horst Schoeler was smoking a cigar over his motorcycle and beat his brown-shirted cousin through three oak stall doors with a doubled brass stirrup-leather until four grooms dragged him off.

---

# CHAPTER NINE

## Aleja Szucha 25

**Warsaw — November 9, 1943**

Before nineteen-thirty-nine, Number Twenty-Five Aleja Szucha had housed the Polish Ministry of Religious Denominations and Public Enlightenment—a sleek, pale-limestone monument of nineteen-thirties modernist geometry with wide travertine staircases, brass handrails, and tall, airy windows designed to let light fall across school curricula.

The *Sicherheitspolizei* and SD had found the architecture admirably suited to their own enlightenment.

Sitting upright in the rear leather seat of a black Mercedes staff car as iron gates clanged shut behind the rear bumper at nine-forty on Tuesday morning, Sylwia looked once up the sheer limestone cliff of the courtyard and reached unobtrusively with two chilled fingers to touch the collar seam below her left jaw.

The paraffined glass cherry-stone was there. Eight seconds from tooth-crack to loss of consciousness; forty seconds to irreversible cytochrome oxidase blockade.

"Right this way, Frau Doktor," murmured *Kriminalsekretär* Krauss politely, holding the car door against thin November snow-flurries that melted instantly on the swept concrete. "Sturmbannführer Reinhardt dislikes keeping busy physicians away from their wards."

Twenty minutes earlier, Krauss and two heavyset subordinates had stepped into Infant Jesus Hospital just as Sylwia finished morning rounds—precisely fifteen minutes after Hauptmann Brandt's courier noted that Oberstleutnant Schoeler had been summoned across the Vistula to Praga rail headquarters for an urgent logistics inspection. No arrest warrant had been presented; Krauss had respected OKH *Sonderausweis* Number One-One-Four to the fastidious letter: *Merely twenty minutes' voluntary witness clarification regarding October pharmacy discrepancies, Frau Doktor; naturally, if you prefer we request formal military detention papers through Berlin...*

Refusing would have confessed terror on the spot. Leaving Halina a single coded sentence at the scrub sink—*"Tell Pan Doktor Majewski the Ujazdów linen delivery is forty minutes early"*—which sent Halina racing out the back coal gate to warn both Marek's lookout and Brandt's telephone exchange on Aleja Róż, Sylwia had put on her charcoal coat without changing out of her white hospital smock beneath and stepped calmly into the black car.

Inside the great marble entrance hall, pristine order reigned. Blonde Reich-German stenographers in neat sweater-sets clicked briskly past on low heels carrying manila folders and trays of fresh Viennese apricot pastries; typewriters rattled steadily behind oak doors; the air smelled of floor wax, roasted coffee—real coffee, an intoxicating, obscene ghost from dead peacetime—and damp wool.

Only as Krauss escorted her past the iron-grilled basement stairwell toward the lift did the other smell drift faintly upward on warm radiator drafts: stale sweat, unwashed terror, raw Lysol poured over concrete, and copper blood. Down in that brightly lit subterranean corridor—every child in Warsaw knew its name in nightmares: *tramwaj*, the "tramway"—two rows of bruised, silent Polish men and women sat upright on backless wooden benches bolted one behind another like streetcar seats, forbidden to turn their heads half an inch left or right while gramophones behind steel doors played cheerful Bavarian brass marches at full volume over the thud of rubber truncheons.

Sylwia did not glance down the stairs. She stepped into the mahogany-paneled paternoster lift, watched three floors glide smoothly past, and walked behind Krauss into Room Three-Fourteen.

Sunlight—thin, wintry, silver—fell across a spacious corner office furnished with a looted Persian Tabriz carpet, an upright black Bechstein piano against the side wall, and a broad walnut desk spotless save for a green leather blotter, a silver tray bearing a porcelain pot of steaming coffee, and a closed buff folder.

Seated at the Bechstein in crisp mouse-grey SD wool, Otto Reinhardt finished the quiet, crystalline closing measure of the *Aria* from Bach's *Goldberg Variations*, let the final G-major chord decay cleanly into silence, and rose with a pleasant smile.

"Forgive a bachelor's morning indulgence, Frau Doktor," Reinhardt said, gesturing courteously toward a leather armchair facing the tall windows—angled, as every detective since Vidocq angled witness chairs, so winter light struck the visitor's irises squarely while leaving the host in comfortable silhouette. "Busoni spoils Bach with pedal, don't you find? Pure counterpoint requires clean fingers—much like vascular surgery. Coffee? Fresh Santos bean from Lisbon via Geneva, and real cream."

"Thank you, no, Herr Sturmbannführer," Sylwia said evenly, sitting upright without leaning against the leather back, gloved hands folded loosely in her lap. "Rationed chicory ruins the stomach for real caffeine; I should have palpitations before noon."

"Admirable self-discipline." Pouring himself half a cup without sugar, Reinhardt seated himself behind the walnut desk, wiped his rimless gold spectacles once with yellow chamois, hooked them neatly behind small pale ears, and opened the buff folder.

Out slid two glossy photostatic sheets bearing the circular eagle stamp of *Polizeipräsidium Berlin — Abteilung Einwohnermeldeamt*, followed by an eight-by-ten photographic plate copied from a school annual.

Reinhardt turned both pages one hundred eighty degrees so Sylwia could read them right-side up.

There, staring up across thirteen years and two ruined capitals from the sunlit steps of Reichstagsufer Six in June nineteen-thirty, stood sixty eighteen- and nineteen-year-olds in stiff white collars and summer dresses. Front row center sat Oberstudiendirektor Becker and sardonic Doktor Glass; two places to Glass's right stood tall, arrogant young **Ludwig Schoeler**; two places to Glass's left, chin tilted high and storm-grey eyes daring the camera lens to blink first, stood **Sylwia Kotarska**. Below the photograph ran the certified Prussian *Reifeprüfung* protocol:

*1. Schoeler, Ludwig Heinrich — 1,00*
*2. Kotarska, Sylwia Maria — 1,00*

Silence ticked gently in the warm room. A gilt mantel clock chimed the quarter-hour.

"When we spoke in Pavilion Three on the morning of October twentieth," Reinhardt remarked mildly, sipping his coffee, "you mentioned four schoolgirl years in Berlin while your late father argued tariff law on Kurfürstenstraße. Memory plays odd tricks under fatigue, of course—yet it does strike a tidy mind as remarkable that you omitted spending three consecutive years in the identical thirty-student classroom with the man bleeding on your operating table."

Sylwia looked down at the nineteen-year-old girl in the photograph for three calm seconds, and then raised eyes colder than Baltic slate straight into Reinhardt's spectacles.

She did not stammer; she did not feign surprise; she let an ugly, razor-edged curve—pure, undiluted June nineteen-thirty Wannsee venom, preserved intact in cold storage inside her ribs for thirteen winters and requiring zero acting whatsoever—touch one corner of her mouth.

"Had you walked into Pavilion Three at seven in the morning and asked me whether I recognized the bastard I had just spent ninety minutes keeping alive at gunpoint, Herr Sturmbannführer, what answer did you expect?" Sylwia asked dryly. "If I announced to Hauptmann Brandt and yourself that Ludwig Schoeler and I despised the sight of each other across a school bench in nineteen-thirty, and that he died—suppose a stitch gave way Thursday—whose neck would your firing squad have stretched first?"

Reinhardt tilted his head appreciatively, intrigued rather than displeased by the absence of female flutter. "Despised, Frau Doktor? Young people tied at one-point-zero-zero across three years rarely remain indifferent."

"Indifferent?" Sylwia let out a short, bitter laugh so wholly genuine the hairs could have stood up on Ludwig's neck had he been in the room. "Ask SS-Obersturmbannführer Horst Schoeler on Prinz-Albrecht-Straße in Berlin how 'fond' the Schoeler family and Counselor Kotarski's daughter were of one another! Call Berlin on your teleprinter right now—Horst Schoeler will remember June twenty-first, nineteen-thirty on the Wannsee pier with great fondness!"

Behind his rimless lenses, Reinhardt's pale eyes sharpened half a millimeter. Offering an active senior SS officer on Prinz-Albrecht-Straße as a character witness was the last move on earth an underground conspirator hiding collusion ever dared make.

"Enlighten me," Reinhardt invited softly.

"Gladly," Sylwia said, voice ringing flat and hard as iron filings. "For three years Ludwig Schoeler could not stomach a Polish girl matching his marks in front of Prussian masters. When we tied down to the hundredth decimal on the *Abitur*, his father—Generalmajor Heinrich Schoeler—pulled strings at the Ministry so a Slav wouldn't carry the Humboldt Medal out of Berlin. And on graduation night at Wannsee, in front of his cousin Horst and two Berlin policemen, Ludwig Schoeler threw a ten-Reichsmark silver piece at my shoes to settle a dormitory wager that he could humiliate Counselor Kotarski's daughter before our diplomatic train left at dawn. Nine years later, men in uniform broke my father's hands in this very building and shipped his ashes home in a cardboard box."

She leaned two inches forward toward the green blotter, storm-grey eyes blazing cold into Reinhardt's face.

"So no, Herr Sturmbannführer—I did not gossip over bedpans about old school days. When Hauptmann Brandt shoved a Luger under old Professor Majewski's chin and your office threatened to murder a hundred Poles plus my nurses if Schoeler died, I sewed his artery shut because I am a surgeon and I do not murder patients—not even a Schoeler. And when he woke up choking on blood and forced to owe every breath in his proud Prussian lungs to my hands, I didn't mind keeping him under my roof one bit."

For ten long seconds Otto Reinhardt studied every muscle around her mouth and eyes, tapping one clean fingernail silently against the porcelain saucer.

It hung together. Psychologically, historically, down to verifiable names in RSHA Berlin, every joint of her story locked seamlessly—*almost* too seamlessly. And if Oberstleutnant Schoeler had afterward slapped an OKH requisition pass on her out of perverse aristocratic dominance—forcing the proud Polish enemy who saved him to trot into Number Twelve Aleja Róż every evening at six o'clock like an obedient housemaid changing bandages—why, Reinhardt knew half a dozen Prussian staff colonels vain enough to relish precisely that little cruelty.

*Unless,* whispered the patient Munich detective behind Reinhardt's freckled forehead, *the lady protests one shade too brilliantly.*

"Most illuminating, Frau Doktor," Reinhardt sighed, closing the buff folder neatly. "Prussian military families can be insufferably provincial, as a Bavarian like myself knows all too well. In fact, we need detain you no longer—save for one tiny medical courtesy on your way past the ground floor."

Every instinct along Sylwia's spine screamed warning, though her face remained carved alabaster as Reinhardt rose and picked up his cap.

"Our night patrol near the Bródno railway marshaling yards picked up an armed Polish courier at three this morning," Reinhardt chatted pleasantly as they rode the lift downward—past the sunny ground-floor exit, straight down one level lower into the concrete windowless basement. "Stubborn boy. Dislocated shoulder and two cracked ribs resisting arrest, and my overzealous assistants lack clinical subtlety. Tell me whether his heart will tolerate another hour's questioning before noon—our own police physician is testifying in Cracow today—and Krauss will drive you straight back to Lindleya."

Down here the Bavarian brass march blaring from a Bakelite loudspeaker bolted near the ceiling—*Alte Kameraden*, needle scratching rhythmically in worn shellac grooves—could not drown a hoarse, repetitive animal whimper coming from Cell Six, nor the choking chemical stench of Lysol, scorched hair, and voided bowels. Along the center of the whitewashed corridor sat fourteen motionless men and women on the wooden "tramway" benches staring straight ahead at white brick. None breathed loud enough to hear.

Krauss unlocked Iron Door Four at the end of the passage.

Inside a ten-by-twelve concrete wash-cell lit by an unshaded two-hundred-watt bulb dangling on twisted black cord, two shirt-sleeved SD interrogators with rolled cuffs stood smoking cigarettes beside a zinc bucket of pink water and a table laid with steel carpenter's pincers, braided bull-pizzles, and a hand-cranked field magneto.

Strapped half-upright across an inclined wet wooden slaughtering trestle by leather cuffs at both ankles and his left wrist—his right wrist hung temporarily unbuckled in a pool of clotting dark blood where four fingernails had been wrenched raggedly out of the nail-beds down to bare white phalanx bone—lay a slight nineteen-year-old boy stripped to tattered blood-soaked trousers.

His snub Powiśle nose was broken sideways across swollen purple cheeks; burns from magneto clips charred black circles around both nipples; his left eye was hammered shut into a livid plum slit.

*Tomek.*

*Wróbel.*

The freckled nineteen-year-old printer's apprentice who had whistled behind her through Kercelak mud, leaped off brick rubble onto a German gendarme's shoulders in Sienna Alley to save her life, and carried twenty messages between her cellar and Marek's hands.

Reinhardt did not look at the boy on the trestle at all. Standing two paces to Sylwia's right rear, Otto Reinhardt watched *exclusively* the side of Sylwia Kotarska's throat, her eyelids, and the radial edge of her bare hands as she stepped under the glaring two-hundred-watt bulb.

Inside Sylwia's chest, something tore silently from breastbone to spine—yet thirty thousand hours of holding retractors while children died on zinc tables kept her eyelids, nostrils, and fingertips frozen in absolute, terrifying stillness. She stripped off her leather street gloves, dropped them neatly into her coat pocket without haste, and stepped directly beside the bloody wooden trestle.

"Uncover his chest completely and turn off that gramophone outside if you want me to auscultate heart sounds without a stethoscope," she said briskly in clipped German.

At the sound of her German voice, Tomek's battered head stirred half an inch on the wet wood.

Slowly, painfully, his single unswollen right eye—clear hazel-brown under matted, blood-clotted sandy hair—slitted open against the blinding overhead bulb and focused twelve inches upward onto Sylwia's pale, expressionless face above her open charcoal coat and white hospital smock.

For one microscopic tenth of a second—invisible to Reinhardt standing behind her right shoulder because Sylwia kept her head angled cleanly between them—she saw shock flash across that single hazel eye, followed instantly by fierce, soaring comprehension: *They haven't got her yet. They're testing her on me.*

Sylwia laid her cool, bare right palm flat over his burned left precordium—letting him feel, through steady skin contact alone, all the love and grief and gratitude she dared never speak aloud on earth—while sliding her left fingers lightly along his purpled fifth and sixth ribs. Subcutaneous air crackled beneath her fingertips: broken rib puncturing lung.

"Left pneumothorax and flail fifth rib," she reported clinically over her shoulder. "Blood pressure is—"

Before she finished the word, Tomek dragged one rasping, blood-bubbling breath into his punctured lung, twisted his swollen purple mouth into a ferocious Powiśle gutter sneer, and spat a thick gobbet of crimson blood and broken tooth enamel squarely onto the white starched lapel of Sylwia's hospital smock.

*"Weź te szwabskie łapy, kurwo!"* Tomek croaked in loud, raw Warsaw street Polish that both bilingual SD thugs by the wall understood effortlessly. *"Take your Kraut-whore paws off me, bitch! Go suck off your German colonels upstairs—Poland shaves sluts like you bald before the rope!"*

One of the shirt-sleeved interrogators lunged forward roaring *"Verfluchter Hund!"* with a raised bull-pizzle to smash the boy's teeth in.

And Tomek—whose bloody, fingerless right hand was free and whose left wrist-strap the guards had loosened two notches ten minutes earlier when reviving him with a bucket of icy water—used that guard's step blocking Reinhardt's line of sight to snatch his left wrist clean through the wet leather buckle.

He didn't grab for a throat or a weapon. Nineteen years old, carrying nineteen underground street addresses and the true name of *Kora* behind bruised bone while magneto wires waited on the table for afternoon session, Tomek threw every remaining ounce of Powiśle muscle sideways off the high wooden trestle—diving headfirst, hands tucked voluntarily behind his own ribs so reflex wouldn't break his fall, driving the right temple of his skull and hyperextended neck with sickening, full-body velocity straight down onto the heavy, square cast-iron wheel-flange of the main steam-radiator pipe bolted six inches above the concrete floor.

The sound—a wet, crunching *THOCK-snap* like an axe splitting green oak—stopped every breath in Cell Four.

Tomek's thin legs jerked violently once, heels drumming twice against the zinc water bucket; bright pink cerebrospinal-and-lung froth bubbled softly from his nostrils onto dusty concrete; then every muscle went profoundly, mercifully slack.

"Scheiße—!" cursed Krauss, shoving past the guard as Sylwia knelt smoothly in the spreading dark pool beside the radiator pipe without hitching her wool skirt out of the blood, thumb lifting Tomek's limp eyelid—pupil blown wide, fixed, black, free forever of pincers and wires and Aleja Szucha—while two fingers confirmed zero carotid pulse beneath his broken jaw.

*Sleep easy, little Sparrow,* whispered her silent soul behind iron ribs while hot blood soaked slowly through the knees of her stockings on cold concrete. *God keep you where no dogs bark.*

Rising without haste, wiping Tomek's blood off her fingertips with a square of gauze from her pocket rather than trembling for a sink, Sylwia turned calmly toward Otto Reinhardt.

Reinhardt's pale freckled face showed neither outrage nor pity—only mild, fastidious irritation at untidy subordinates.
