Yet as his eyes searched Sylwia's face beneath the pitiless bare bulb, looking at Tomek's bloody spittle drying red across her white lapel and her rock-steady grey eyes, even Munich's sharpest hound found nothing whatsoever to sink teeth into.

"Depressed right temporal fracture with atlanto-occipital dislocation," Sylwia stated coldly. "Instantaneous brainstem arrest. If your men leave greased slaughtering benches unstrapped beside iron steam valves, Herr Sturmbannführer, you scarcely need physicians."

Before Reinhardt could reply, rapid boots clattered down the iron cellar stairs and a pale young SD *Unterscharführer* leaned breathlessly into the doorway, saluting sharply:

"Herr Sturmbannführer—forgive the interruption! Duty desk upstairs reports an armed detachment of Wehrmacht *Feldgendarmerie* has just entered the front courtyard from Praga, and Oberstleutnant Schoeler is in the main vestibule demanding—"

"Demanding nothing, Reinhardt," cut a cold, carrying baritone directly behind the young corporal's shoulder as tall boots rang firmly down the final three concrete steps into the basement corridor itself. "Informing."

Every head in the cellar passage—even two bruised shadows on the end of the "tramway" bench before guards barked their chins straight—stiffened at the apparition standing beneath the low whitewashed vault.

Dressed in freshly pressed field-grey General Staff uniform with broad crimson *Karmesinrot* collar tabs and trouser stripes blazing under the bare bulbs, peaked cap set razor-level above his pale, hawk-boned face, left arm out of its visible sling and hidden only by resting two gloved fingers casually inside the second button of his tunic just like Scharnhorst in an oil painting, stood Oberstleutnant Ludwig Schoeler—flanked one pace behind by scarred Hauptmann Brandt and two six-foot Wehrmacht military police sergeants wearing silver gorgets over slung submachine guns.

How he had crossed two Vistula bridges from Praga inside twenty-eight minutes and walked up steps with an ununited collarbone without blacking out, Sylwia could only guess by the dead-white line bracketing his mouth and the tiny beads of cold sweat gleaming beneath his cap visor. His slate-blue eyes swept once—for less than a fifth of a second—into Cell Four: taking in the dead boy crumpled beside the radiator pipe, the bloody spittle drying on Sylwia's white hospital lapel, and the crimson circles soaking both knees of her dark stockings.

Nothing flickered in his face—yet the temperature in the cellar dropped twenty degrees.

"Oberstleutnant Schoeler," Reinhardt said smoothly, stepping out into the corridor and pulling Cell Four's iron door half-shut behind him while offering a courteous two-inch bow. "Remarkable recovery. We understood Praga rail inspection occupied your morning."

"Praga occupies ten minutes when Berlin teleprinters clatter," Ludwig replied icily, drawing a folded pink OKH top-priority *Blitzfernschreiben* flimsy from his leather gauntlet cuff and holding it six inches from Reinhardt's spectacles without surrendering possession of the paper. "Signed forty minutes ago at Mauerwald by Generalmajor Gehlen and countersigned by Chief of Army Feldpolizei: all medical and civilian personnel covered under *Sonderausweis* Series One-Fourteen remain under exclusive OKH counter-espionage cognizance pending completion of the Małkinia convoy inquiry. Any interference—including 'voluntary witness invitations'—requires personal countersignature from Reichsführer-SS Himmler's field liaison at Hochwald."

Reinhardt read the teleprinter stamp silently through his gold-wired lenses. A muscle the size of a grain of rice flexed once behind his neat jaw. Pulling Gehlen's name directly out of *Fremde Heere Ost* meant Schoeler still carried heavier steel in East Prussia than Reinhardt had calculated.

"No interference was dreamed of, Herr Oberstleutnant," Reinhardt purred silkily. "Frau Doktor Kotarska generously cleared up a minor pharmacy question and pronounced a clumsy suicide down here—saving my clerk forty lines of paperwork. She is entirely at your disposal."

"Good," Ludwig clipped. His eyes touched Sylwia with supreme, glacial ownership that made Krauss smirk knowingly behind Reinhardt's back. "Fetch your coat hem out of that filth, Frau Doktor. My pleural dressing is two hours overdue and I have zero intention of letting Ujazdów quacks ruin my afternoon. Good day, Sturmbannführer."

Nobody spoke a single syllable as they climbed out of the cellar stench into thin, whirling November snow, nor inside the closed Mercedes rolling south down Aleje Ujazdowskie with Brandt sitting rigid beside the driver beyond the glass partition.

Sylwia sat bolt upright staring blindly out at snow dusting the leafless lime trees, hands clenched inside her leather gloves until scars threatened to split, tasting Lysol and seeing nineteen-year-old hazel eyes smiling before a skull hit cast iron. Beside her on the grey broadcloth seat, Ludwig sat equally motionless, left shoulder braced stiffly against every cobblestone jolt, breathing shallow and silent through his nose.

Only when the iron garden gates of Number Twelve Aleja Róż clanged shut behind them, and Brandt dismissed both driver and sentries to the gatehouse before vanishing discreetly into the ground-floor cipher room, did Ludwig walk ahead of her up the wide curving oak stairs of the empty Danish villa, kick shut the heavy double doors of his second-floor bedroom-study, turn the brass key in the lock, and lean one hand heavily onto the mahogany table as his knees nearly buckled.

---

# CHAPTER TEN

## Fever Pitch

**Warsaw — November 9, 1943**

Inside the locked second-floor study of Number Twelve Aleja Róż, coal hissed softly behind the iron grate, snow ticked like dry salt against tall double-glazed casements overlooking leafless garden chestnuts, and for five seconds neither of them moved.

Leaning his right fist heavily on the polished mahogany table until white returned slowly to his lips, Ludwig lifted his left hand gingerly out from between his tunic buttons and reached up to remove his peaked cap. Above his left breast pocket, just beside the silver rim of his Iron Cross First Class, a dark, wet stain the size of a plum had soaked straight through heavy field-grey wool.

Across the room, standing motionless beside the closed oak doors in her open charcoal coat, Sylwia looked down at her own hands.

Under the edges of two fingernails, missed by dry gauze down in Cell Four, clung a hairline crust of nineteen-year-old Tomek's blood. Lower down, across the white starched lapel of her hospital smock, his last defiant spittle had dried brownish-crimson, and both knees of her dark wool-rayon stockings stuck cold and stiff against her patellae where she had knelt beside the cast-iron steam valve.

*Crack-thock.*

Suddenly—without warning, after holding thirty thousand pounds of cold granite across her chest under Otto Reinhardt's spectacles—a violent, shuddering convulsion ran from the base of Sylwia's skull straight down into her knees, clicking her back teeth audibly together once before she clamped her jaw shut so fiercely muscles corded along her throat.

"Sit down before you hit the floorboards," Ludwig said roughly, starting around the table toward her.

"Don't touch me!" Her voice lashed across the room like cold whip-wire. Spinning away from his outstretched hand, she dropped her satchel onto a chair, snatched the brass hooks of his field-grey tunic collar open in two ferocious jerks before he could do it one-handed, and peeled the heavy crimson-tabbed wool carefully down off his braced left shoulder and right arm.

Beneath the tunic, half the left front of his white linen shirt was bright wet scarlet.

"Are you completely insane?!" Sylwia hissed, hands steady again only because fury and surgical reflex flooded into the breach before grief could drown her. Unbuttoning his shirt swiftly and peeling blood-stuck linen off the adhesive strapping below his collarbone, she saw at once what marching upright into Gestapo headquarters without a sling had cost: thank God, no deep pulsatile hematoma from the subclavian artery itself, only the lateral two cutaneous silk mattress stitches torn clean through granulating skin edge from shoulder-girdle tension, bleeding briskly down his bare ribs. "Two weeks post-resection and you rip off your Velpeau sling to play Prussian peacock down cellar stairs?! Five millimeters deeper and you'd have ruptured the anastomosis right in front of Reinhardt's desk!"

"Had I stayed here in a sling waiting on routine channels," Ludwig shot back, jaw set hard as she slapped a dry gauze pad roughly against his bleeding collarbone, "you'd still be downstairs in Cell Four watching Reinhardt's apes pull fingernails out of the next boy on the bench until you blinked!"

"I didn't blink!" Something wild and jagged tore loose behind her storm-grey eyes—four years of silent cellar graves, Zbigniew's bare foot twitching in red sand, Tomek's hazel eye smiling half a second before his skull split open against iron, all colliding head-on with this maddening, impossible man bleeding onto her fingers. "He watched my eyes from twenty-four inches away while a nineteen-year-old boy who saved my life ten days ago spat in my face and smashed his own brains out on a steam pipe so they couldn't use pliers to drag *my* name out of his mouth! Do you hear me, Schoeler?! Nineteen years old! And five minutes after I pronounced him dead in his own blood, you come marching down the stairs in crimson stripes waving Gehlen's warrant like I'm your prized hunting bitch on a Berlin chain!"

"That warrant kept you walking out the front gate in daylight instead of vanishing into Pawiak!"

"Reinhardt already had the Reichstagsufer file open on his blotter!" she cried softly, dangerously, shoving the bloody pad hard enough against his skin edge to make his breath hiss sharp between his teeth. "Eight-by-ten glossy plate of *Oberprima* nineteen-thirty sitting right beside his coffee pot! *One point zero zero to one point zero zero, Frau Doktor—tell me, did you love him or try to kill him?*"

Ludwig went dead still under her hands. "What did you tell him?"

Sylwia laughed—low, raw, jagged as broken ampoule glass—lifting blazing grey eyes three inches from his pale face.

"Didn't even have to invent a lie, Herr Oberstleutnant! Isn't that the beauty of Schoeler thoroughness? I told him to wire your cousin SS-Obersturmbannführer Horst Schoeler on Prinz-Albrecht-Straße and ask about June twenty-first, nineteen-thirty on the Wannsee pier!" Her voice dropped to pure, shaking acid, thirteen festering years ripping open like an unlanced abscess down to bare bone. "I told him how your general father stole the Humboldt Medal behind closed doors, and how you tossed a ten-Reichsmark silver piece at my satin shoes in front of Horst's brownshirt thugs to pay off Kurt von Bredow's dormitory wager on breaking Polish mares! Reinhardt believed every syllable—because why wouldn't a Polish surgeon relish carving open the man who treated her like a ten-mark whore at nineteen?!"

For three heartbeats the silence in the room was so total snow could be heard sliding off the copper windowsill outside.

Every trace of color drained out of Ludwig Schoeler's lean face until the thin white crescent scar above his right eyebrow vanished against skin just as pale.

"Ten marks," he repeated very softly. "Thirteen years—and you actually believed I bet ten marks with Kurt von Bredow?"

"I stood on the planks in crimson silk and watched the silver spin at my feet while Horst laughed!"

With a single violent sweep of his good right arm, Ludwig snatched his discarded field-grey tunic off the chair back, reached inside the blood-stiffened inner left breast pocket directly over his heart, wrenched out a battered, sweat-darkened calfskin field wallet creased by four campaigns, and slammed it open upside-down onto the mahogany table.

Paper rations and identity slips fluttered aside as two heavy, tarnished pieces of silver struck the polished wood with a sharp double *clack-ring* and skidded to a halt beside the porcelain washbasin.

Sylwia's breath stopped dead in her throat.

Spinning slowly to rest against the mahogany lay a scarred, two-ounce silver disc stamped **PRIMUS OMNIUM — COLLÈGE FRANÇAIS BERLIN 1930**, its rim dented deeply on the left edge where a nine-millimeter jacket fragment or shattered Horch door-glass had struck it inside his breast pocket on the night of October nineteenth and deflected two centimeters upward into his collarbone instead of straight through his heart.

And nestled directly beside it, tarnished dark pewter-grey in the crevices yet unmistakable down to every chased silver willow-vein, lay Cécile Moreau-Kotarska's antique Mazovian leaf hair-clasp.

Sylwia stared down at the wood as if the floor had dropped away beneath her clogs. Slowly, involuntarily, one bare blood-crusty fingertip reached halfway toward her mother's silver leaf without quite touching it.

"Where—" Her voice cracked into an unrecognizable whisper. "That fell in the dark—you threw the medal into six feet of lake water—"

" *You* threw the medal into six feet of lake water!" Ludwig snarled low in his throat, thirteen years of silent, swallowed agony erupting straight through Prussian iron as he stepped flat in front of her, shirt hanging torn and crimson-soaked off one bare shoulder, slate-blue eyes burning black-centered down into hers. "I dove seven times naked into Wannsee mud at two in the morning after your cab cleared the gate until my fingers bled finding it! Do you want to know who those two men in trench coats standing behind Horst on the pier were, you blind, proud, infuriating fool?!"

She couldn't speak—only stared up into his blazing eyes while the walls of the room tilted.

"Abteilung One-A—Berlin Political Police!" Ludwig rasped, gripping the edge of the mahogany table beside her hip with his right hand so hard his forearm tendons stood out like steel cables. "Your father had just handed the French Military Attaché documented serial numbers on illegal *Reichswehr* howitzer depots in Pomerania forty-eight hours earlier! Horst wasn't strolling down to look at boats—they came down that path looking to catch Counselor Kotarski's eighteen-year-old daughter half-undressed in the dark, haul you into Alexanderplatz lockup overnight on a morals charge so your father missed the seven-forty diplomatic courier train, and tear his unsealed trunks apart at Friedrichstraße Station while my father stood thirty yards up the path watching!"

Sylwia pressed the back of her hand hard against her parted lips, remembering—with blinding, sickening clarity across thirteen years—the trench-coated voice muttering outside the cedar slats: *haul them both down to Precinct Twelve until ten tomorrow morning... old Kotarski misses the seven-forty express... twelve clean hours to search his unsealed baggage!* In the terror and confusion of the storm, she had heard only fragments before Horst pounded on the oak door.

"Had I fought three armed policemen on that dock or played romantic hero defending your honor," Ludwig breathed fiercely, leaning down until his forehead nearly touched hers, "neither you nor your father would have crossed the Polish border that week! Giving Horst an ugly laugh over ten marks turned you from diplomatic leverage into conquered schoolboy trash not worth ten lines of precinct ink! And at two-thirty that morning I walked into the stables with a doubled brass stirrup-leather and broke four of Horst's ribs and his collarbone so badly he spent August in the Charité!"

A dry, soundless sob wrenched Sylwia's ribs—not delicate, tearing upward from somewhere buried under nineteen-thirty, under Sachsenhausen's cardboard ash-box, under Tomek's broken skull on concrete ten yards away an hour ago.

"Thirteen years," she whispered blindly, grey eyes swimming abruptly in scalding, unshed salt she still fought furiously not to spill. "Thirteen years I hated you every winter I woke up—"

"Good," Ludwig whispered harshly, raising his uninjured right hand—ignoring the fresh blood trickling warm down his own bare chest from the popped collarbone stitch—to cup the side of her icy jaw, thumb brushing fiercely across her cheekbone. "Hating me kept your spine iron down in that cellar today. Now hold still before that boy's blood freezes onto your skin."

Before she could protest, he unbuttoned her charcoal street coat one-handed and slid it off her shoulders onto the chair; next, hooking steady fingers at the hem and collar buttons of her blood-spattered white hospital smock—careful around the left collar seam where her cyanide bead slept—he unbuttoned it down the front and peeled the stained white linen away, tossing it toward the hearth corner. Dropping onto one knee on the Turkish rug directly in front of her despite the sharp hitch in his strapped left shoulder, Ludwig unhooked both garter clasps beneath her dark wool skirt hem and rolled the stiff, blood-soaked stockings gently down off her chilled knees and calves, stripping her shoes and ruined hosiery away until she stood barefoot on the warm carpet before the coal fire in her plain dark skirt and thin sleeveless white cotton chemise.

Pouring warm water from the brass hearth kettle into the porcelain washbasin, Ludwig wrung out a clean linen towel, knelt back down before her bare legs, and washed every trace of Aleja Szucha's cellar—dark crimson circles over both kneecaps, specks along her shins—clean off her pale skin with slow, methodical, almost reverent roughness. Rising again, he took both her cold hands into the warm water basin and scrubbed gently beneath each surgeon's fingernail with a soft bristle brush and soap until the water ran pink and her hands emerged spotless.

Standing barefoot in thin cotton six inches from his open, blood-streaked chest while coal-light flickered orange across his bent tarnished-gold head, Sylwia felt the last wall inside her give way cleanly down into the flood.

"Sit on the edge of the desk before you bleed all over my clean hands," she whispered raggedly, pulling her surgical needle-roll out of her bag on legs that barely held weight.

Ludwig sat back against the heavy mahogany edge without a word. No Novocain this time—neither of them cared about skin sting. Threading a curved cutting needle with black braided silk in three seconds flat, standing squarely between his parted General Staff riding breeches so she could reach his left infraclavicular wound cleanly in the firelight, Sylwia dabbed the oozing gap dry with alcohol—he didn't flinch a muscle—drove two swift, tight interrupted sutures through skin and subcuticular fascia, tied three square throws, and snipped the tails with her scissors.

When she laid the scissors down on the mahogany behind his hip, her hands were still resting against his bare, hot chest on either side of the fresh white gauze pad—right over the steady, heavy *lub-dup, lub-dup* of the heart she had held between gloved fingers three weeks ago.

Slowly, Sylwia lifted her eyes to his.

Ludwig's good right hand came up off the desk edge, slid slowly around the bare small of her back above her skirt waistband, and pulled her flush between his parted thighs until her thin cotton chemise flattened directly against his bare sternum.

"Tell me to stop," Ludwig said, voice scraped raw to bone in the firelit quiet, looking straight into her storm-grey eyes as his thumb found the rapid, wild flutter at the base of her bare ribs, "and I walk downstairs to Brandt right now and never touch you again until this war ends."

Sylwia didn't say a word.

Reaching up with both clean, trembling hands, she gripped two fistfuls of thick tarnished-gold hair behind his ears, pulled his head down, and kissed his mouth open with everything thirteen stolen years and four years of hell had left alive inside her.

A low groan vibrated straight from Ludwig's chest into hers. Tightening his right arm like an iron band around her waist, he lifted her sideways onto the cleared mahogany edge briefly before catching the side zipper and horn button of her dark wool skirt—it pooled dark around her ankles on the rug as she stepped barefoot out of it—and turning two strides to back her down onto the wide, low sleigh-bed facing the coal grate.

Remembering his shattered left collarbone even while her own blood roared like high tide in her ears, Sylwia caught his good right shoulder before he could braced weight on his left arm, pushing him firmly back against the piled linen bolsters at a forty-five-degree incline while pulling her thin white cotton chemise straight up over her own head and tossing it blind onto the floor, scattering tortoise-shell hairpins across the oak boards as three feet of heavy dark-chestnut hair tumbled loose across her bare shoulders and breasts.

In the orange glow of the anthracite fire, Ludwig's breath caught audibly in his throat.

Nineteen-thirty's coltish girl was gone; in her place knelt a thirty-two-year-old woman pared down by war to breathtaking, sculptural severity—long thighs pale as birch bark, narrow waist showing the faint shadow of starved ribs, high full breasts tipped dark rose in the cool room air, storm-grey eyes fierce and unashamed beneath tumbled dark hair, a pale violet transfusion scar still fading at the inner bend of her left elbow.

"Come here," Ludwig rasped, voice shaking for the first time in his adult life.

Sliding partway down the mattress flat on his spine so his healing left shoulder took zero strain, he hooked his scarred right arm around her bare hips and drew her upward until her trembling knees framed his lean torso, closing his mouth hot and starving first over the transfusion bruise inside her left elbow, kissing down her bare inner arm, across her collarbone—deliberately pressing an open-mouthed, scorching kiss directly over the hollow of her throat where her cyanide collar had lain—before sucking her right nipple deep into the scalding heat of his mouth.

Sylwia arched sharply overhead, gripping the carved mahogany headboard upright with both hands as teeth grazed swollen flesh and rough evening golden stubble abraded the tender underswell of both breasts. Thirteen years without this—even those brief winter nights with Marek in forty had been hurried, desperate creature-comfort under wool blankets in the dark, light-years removed from this terrifying, total annihilation of every barrier beneath her skin.

"Higher," Ludwig ordered roughly against her wet breastbone, right hand gripping the firm curve of her buttock and lifting her effortlessly upward along the mattress until her knees rested on either side of his shoulders—or rather pivoting her cleanly onto her back across the center of the wide bed while he shifted onto his healthy right hip and elbow beside her, spreading her long pale legs wide with his broad right palm and dropping his mouth straight down between her thighs.

"Ludwig—!" Sylwia gasped, hands flying down from the headboard to tangle convulsively in his cropped golden hair, back bowing clean off the linen sheet as his wide, hot tongue licked one flat, unhurried stroke from base to swollen apex through slick, aching folds already drenched before he touched her.

No tentative nineteen-year-old boy in a dark boathouse anymore: he devoured her with ruthless, deliberate, shattering mastery, holding her bucking hips pinned effortlessly to the mattress under one broad scarred forearm across her lower belly while his tongue circled, flicked, and sucked hard over bare screaming nerves, sliding two long calloused fingers deep up inside her clenching heat—curving upward against the exact front wall that made black-and-gold stars explode behind her squeezed eyelids.

Remembering Brandt downstairs in the cipher room even as everything inside her tore loose from gravity, Sylwia jammed the knuckle of her own left wrist hard between her teeth to stifle a raw, keening cry, thighs clamping viselike around Ludwig's jaw and ears as a violent, endless orgasm ripped upward from the pit of her womb through her spine, leaving her gasping raggedly into her bitten wrist while aftershocks spasmed mercilessly around his buried fingers.

Rising swiftly onto his knees beside her hip while she still trembled half-blind in the firelight, Ludwig kicked off boots and crimson-striped General Staff breeches one-handed—leaving only the white adhesive strapping and fresh collarbone gauze across his lean, battle-scarred torso, the purple Kursk mortar scar livid along his right flank, his cock jutting thick, heavy, and dark-flushed against his flat belly—and lay back flat against the bolsters, catching her right wrist to pull her up astride him so zero weight fell across his healing left clavicle.

"On me, Sylwia," he breathed, slate-blue eyes black as pitch in the orange fireglow, chest heaving fast beneath crisp golden hair. "Look at me when you take me. Thirteen years is long enough in the dark."

Shaking from knees to fingertips, dark hair falling like a heavy curtain forward over both their bare shoulders, Sylwia rose up on her knees straddling his lean thighs, reached down between their bodies with steady surgeon's fingers to wrap around the scorching, velvety-hard thickness of him, guided the broad, slick crown squarely to her drenched, still-pulsing entrance, and sank slowly downward—inch by stretching, breathtaking, explicit inch—until thirty-two years of pride, exile, blood, and war vanished as she sheathed him completely to the hilt.

Both of them groaned aloud into each other's open mouths—deep, guttural, animal.

For three full heartbeats neither dared move, stretched almost past bearing around him, feeling every thick vein pulse deep inside her belly while Ludwig's right hand gripped her bare hipbone hard enough to leave five faint bruises by morning, his forehead thrown back against the bolster, jaw clenched tight in sheer, blinding sensory overload.

"God Almighty," Ludwig rasped through his teeth, lifting his head to stare straight into her dilated grey eyes six inches away as she braced her right palm carefully over his uninjured right pectoral—never near the left suture—and lifted her hips slowly upward almost to the tip before driving herself back down hard onto him. "Sylwia—*meine Sylwia*—"

The German possessive broke the last leash in both of them.

Riding him first in long, rolling, carnal strokes that dragged friction across every raw nerve inside her, then faster—desperate, fierce, wet skin slapping softly against skin in the firelit silence—Sylwia bent low over his healthy right shoulder as Ludwig planted both bare heels flat into the mattress and drove upward from his lean hips to meet every downward slam of her body, his free right hand sliding between their sweat-slick stomachs to press his thumb hard against her swollen clitoris in savage counterpoint to his deep, relentless thrusts.

"Don't close your eyes—" she gasped wildly against his mouth, sinking her teeth hard enough into the muscle of his good right shoulder to leave crescent marks beside her lips as the second wave coiled tighter and hotter than death inside her pelvis—and Ludwig dragged his mouth back onto hers, swallowing her muffled cry whole as her inner walls clamped down in violent, milking spasms that triggered his own release half a second later: hips driving upward off the sheets with a harsh, strangled groan torn from the bottom of his lungs, pulsing hot and deep inside her again and again until strength went clean out of both their spines at once.

Careful to the very edge of consciousness not to crush his left collarbone, Sylwia rolled breathlessly off onto his good right side, curving her sweat-slick naked body tight along his warm flank while Ludwig pulled the heavy goose-down quilt up over both their bare shoulders with his right arm, tucking her head squarely into the hollow of his right collarbone where her cheek rested directly over the slowing thunder of his heart.

Outside the double casements, Warsaw's November snow thickened steadily in the early dusk, muffling German boots down on Aleja Róż to ghosts.

For a long time neither spoke. Coal settled red and quiet behind the iron bars. Slowly, Ludwig's fingers traced up and down the bare curve of her spine—counting ribs too sharp from four years of occupation turnips—until he felt a single hot, silent drop wet the bare skin over his right pectoral.

Only one; and she hated even that one, wiping her eyelashes fiercely against his shoulder.

"His name was Tomek," Sylwia whispered into the dark hollow of his throat. "Nineteen. Printed underground handbills before his shop burned. Ten days ago in Sienna Alley he jumped off a wall with a piece of piano wire so two gendarmes wouldn't rape and hang me. Today he spat his own blood onto my coat so Reinhardt wouldn't guess he knew my face, and broke his own neck on an iron pipe."

Ludwig did not offer empty soldier's platitudes. His right arm tightened slowly, fiercely around her bare shoulders, pressing his lips hard into the crown of her dark hair and holding her until the silent shaking in her ribs finally stilled.

When her breathing lengthened half an hour later—not asleep, never wholly asleep before curfew—she lifted her chin slightly in the red ember-light and looked up at the clean, severe line of his jaw and the faint white scar above his right brow.

"Why did they send you to Warsaw, Ludwig?" she whispered. "Not what you told Reinhardt. The truth."

For five seconds Ludwig stared up at the shadowed ceiling plaster, turning thirty men's necks over in his palm—and then decided, with the same total ruthlessness that had jumped naked into Wannsee water at nineteen, that keeping half-ledgers with Sylwia Kotarska was finished forever.

"Wait till you hear it," he murmured very quietly against her hair. "Because if Reinhardt hears one whisper of what I'm about to put in your hands, he won't need Pawiak—Berlin will hang me on piano wire before they shoot you."

---

# CHAPTER ELEVEN

## The Warsaw Chessboard

**Warsaw — Late November 1943**

At five-thirty in the morning, thirty minutes before Warsaw's six-o'clock curfew siren wailed across the frozen roofs of Śródmieście, the world outside the blackout curtains was pitch-black and fourteen degrees below freezing.

Sylwia stood before the wavy gilt mirror above the washstand in a borrowed Wehrmacht woolen overcoat of Brandt's—her blood-stained hospital smock lay burned to grey ash inside the coal grate—coiling her heavy dark-chestnut hair back into its severe surgeon's knot at her nape. Behind her in the amber glow of a single shaded bedside lamp, Ludwig sat upright on the edge of the sleigh-bed buttoning a fresh white shirt one-handed over the new adhesive strapping she had just laid across his left collarbone.

Every muscle in Sylwia's inner thighs and back carried a faint, delicious, treacherous ache whenever she shifted weight; on the mahogany table beside her elbow gleamed her mother's tarnished silver Mazovian leaf-clasp, which she refused to wear openly into Polish streets lest Halina or Marek recognize pre-war silver, tucking it safely inside the chamois lining of her surgical roll instead.

"Say that once more," Sylwia said quietly into the mirror glass, pausing with a steel hairpin between her fingers as her storm-grey eyes met Ludwig's reflection behind her shoulder. "Slowly."

Ludwig fastened his cuff button with his teeth and right thumb, stood up barefoot on the Turkish carpet, and walked over behind her—stopping six inches back without touching her shoulders, because when he touched her neither of them thought straight, and what he was putting into her skull weighed thirty men's necks.

"Half the rail-timetable intelligence your London transmitters call *Kronos* never came from Polish track-walkers at Bródno or Siedlce," Ludwig said in a voice pitched barely louder than the coal settling in the grate. "Your observers confirm axle counts, yes—valuable tactical work. Yet the high-level ten-day advance schedules of ammunition and Panther tank replacements moving east through Warsaw toward Orscha and Vitebsk came straight out of German General Staff teleprinters at Smolensk and Minsk."

Sylwia turned slowly around from the mirror to face him. "No Polish agent sits inside Army Group Centre's cipher room."

"No Pole," Ludwig agreed calmly. "German staff officers put them into the pipe—men around Henning von Tresckow and Oster's old *Abwehr* circle."

For four full seconds the silence in the room was absolute. Sylwia was a diplomat's daughter before she was a surgeon; she didn't need childish explanations of what Prussian General Staff officers deliberately leaking Wehrmacht rail movements meant—nor what awaited them in Berlin's Plötzensee prison if caught.

"Why?" she asked starkly. "Prussian colonels didn't suddenly fall in love with Stalin or Poland."

"No," Ludwig answered without a grain of false sentimentality—which was why she believed every syllable. "If you wait for Prussian colonels to weep for Warsaw, you'll wait till Judgment Day. They did it because Hitler is an apocalyptic madman bleeding three million men white on the Dnieper while Himmler's *Einsatzgruppen* turn the German name into leprosy for two centuries. Until Hitler is dead—blown to pieces in his own briefing hut, which Tresckow already tried once in March when a British clam-fuse failed to detonate inside a cognac bottle on Hitler's plane—every week Army Group Centre stalls without fresh reserves pushes the Wehrmacht closer to forcing a regime collapse before the Red Army reaches East Prussia."

Sylwia's mind raced across the hidden geometry of the board, pieces clicking into terrifying alignment. "And Otto Reinhardt?"

"Three weeks ago," Ludwig said, eyes cold as winter steel, "one of Tresckow's couriers got sloppy routing micro-schedules through a Baltic timber merchant in Praga into your Polish *Kronos* network, assuming London radio got warnings out faster without leaving German wireless tracks. Reinhardt caught two outer *Kronos* couriers—and instead of arresting them cleanly, left them loose while using his mole *Jaskółka* to trace the chain upward. Do you see the abyss now, Sylwia?"

"If Reinhardt rolls up *Kronos* from our Warsaw end," she whispered, cold dread tightening her ribs, "he doesn't just shoot our *Oddział II* command—he finds German Generalstab serial watermarks at the bottom of the well."

"And hands Kaltenbrunner and Himmler the rope to hang Tresckow, Canaris, and every surviving officer in Berlin capable of putting a bomb under Hitler's table," Ludwig finished grimly. "Which is why Mauerwald sent me here—officially to smash *Kronos* for Zeitzler, unofficially to cut the Smolensk trail cleanly off before Reinhardt reaches it, and put a bullet or a court-martial into *Jaskółka*."

He deliberately gave her zero Berlin names beyond Tresckow—already whispered in Allied rumors—and Canaris; Sylwia noted the omission instantly and respected him for it. What wasn't inside her skull couldn't be ripped out down in Cell Four.

"Who inside Infant Jesus Hospital has access to Pavilion Three's night ward schedule, the morphine cabinet, *and* outside telephone lines before curfew?" Ludwig asked.

Sylwia ran the mental roster swiftly, ruthlessly, hating every name she weighed: "Professor Majewski—impossible; his wife died in the thirty-nine bombardment and he risked his neck hiding Jewish pediatricians until forty-two. Halina Nowak—I'd stake my carotid on her before my own shadow. Night porter Władek—carrying AK boys downstairs himself. Which leaves three daytime clerks in admissions, Sister Beata in pharmacy, and..."

She stopped.

*Contracted pinhead pupils under a sixty-watt surgical lamp. Peppermint lozenges on a dry tongue. Panicking when the sergeant mentioned Pawiak. Watching Isolation Two from the corridor.*

"Dr. Roman Karski," Sylwia murmured slowly. "Thirty-five. Morphine user—quarter-grain twice a day, far richer stuff lately than hospital stocks carry. And in July his fiancée—Estera Goldblum, a violinist's daughter he hid in Mokotów for two years—disappeared into the Hotel Polski exchange trap on ulica Długa."

Ludwig's pale eyes lit like cold flint striking iron. "*Hotel Polski.* Reinhardt's own Referat Four department ran the foreign-passport extortion screenings at Hotel Polski in July! By God, Kotarska, there's your lever: morphine and a hostage woman!"

"Suspicion isn't proof," Sylwia warned sharply, pulling on her charcoal street coat now that Brandt had dried and pressed the hem downstairs. "If we accuse Karski wrongly while the real *Jaskółka* sits in admissions, we sign an innocent broken man's death warrant and warn Reinhardt."

"We won't guess," Ludwig said quietly. Reaching up, he tucked one stray lock of dark hair behind her ear—his thumb lingering one hot second along the curve of her jaw, reminding both their bodies abruptly of four hours ago in the firelight—before stepping back as six-o'clock factory sirens began moaning across dark snowy Warsaw. "Give me seventy-two hours to lay salted bait on three plates. Whichever plate Aleja Szucha bites tells us who *Jaskółka* is beyond shadow of doubt. Be careful crossing Marszałkowska today—your own people will have watched you enter my gate yesterday and come out at dawn."

"Let them watch," Sylwia said, lifting her chin as she picked up her black bag. "Until six o'clock tonight, Herr Oberstleutnant."

Twenty minutes after Brandt's driver dropped her two blocks shy of Infant Jesus Hospital, Hauptmann Viktor Brandt climbed upstairs to the study carrying two mugs of steaming ersatz acorn coffee and three freshly typed flimsy sheets stamped *GEHEIME KOMMANDOSACHE — TRANSPORTKOMMANDANTUR WARSCHAU*.

Ludwig stood by the tall mahogany desk in riding breeches and shirt-sleeves, left arm back in its linen sling to rest the collarbone, carefully marking microscopic route variations onto a railway map of eastern Poland with a red Conté pencil.

"All three canary timetables typed, Herr Oberstleutnant," Brandt reported quietly, laying them side by side. "Schedule Alpha routes the Seventeenth Panzer replacement battalion through Siedlce on December fourth at zero-two-hundred—we leave that carbon inside Infant Jesus Hospital's medical disinfection requisition at noon so Doktor Karski and the admissions clerks have clean access to it. Schedule Beta routes the same train through Łuków at zero-four-hundred—passed casually through Wehrmacht Town Major's clerk where SD street watchers drink."

Brandt paused, looking down at the third flimsy sheet in Ludwig's hand.

"And Schedule Gamma?" Brandt asked softly.

On Schedule Gamma, Ludwig had routed the phantom tank train across Bridge Span Four east of Siedlce at twenty-three hundred hours on December third—and while Brandt watched in troubled silence, Ludwig unlocked his personal black calfskin Generalstab dispatch case sitting beside the desk lamp, slid Schedule Gamma beneath the top cipher pad, and turned the small brass key once, dropping the spare key into the porcelain pen-tray behind the inkstand where anyone left alone in this study for ten minutes could lift it, press a wax impression, or turn the lock.

Brandt cleared his throat quietly. "Nobody enters this second-floor room except myself and Frau Doktor Kotarska, Herr Oberstleutnant."

Ludwig did not look up from closing the brass hasp. "Precisely, Viktor."

Scarred East Prussian though he was, Brandt looked almost pained on his colonel's behalf. "After you marched straight into Reinhardt's teeth down in Aleja Szucha yesterday to pull her out? You still lay a barium-meal trap in front of her?"

Ludwig walked to the tall frosted window, looking down through leafless white chestnut branches toward the iron garden gate where Sylwia's narrow boot-prints were already filling softly with fresh November snow.

"Her people lost a courier boy in Reinhardt's cellar yesterday morning, Viktor," Ludwig said, his voice completely level, devoid of illusion yet heavy as lead. "And she stayed behind German gates past curfew last night. Before Friday, Polish Counter-Intelligence will hold a gun to her ribs demanding proof she hasn't gone over to us. When they force her hand—I intend to know about it before Otto Reinhardt does."

***

Inside Pavilion Three at seven-fifteen that morning, the air already smelled of wet boots, carbolic, and Official Germany.

Before Sylwia had even hung her damp charcoal street coat inside the doctors' cloakroom, Halina Nowak intercepted her behind the linen press with dark eyes wide above a tray of boiled catheters.

"Amtsarzt Müller from the German Municipal Health Chamber is in Professor Majewski's office right now with SS-Doktor Krantz," Halina whispered rapidly in Polish while sliding two glass syringes onto cotton. "Arrived twenty minutes after six—demanding to audit every bed-ticket in Recovery, count our ether and morphine ampoules against October admissions, and see your night attendance signature! Professor Majewski already signed your name onto the midnight septic-ward log in left-handed scrawl and told them you went out at five-thirty on a postpartum hemorrhage in Wola!"

Sylwia squeezed Halina's elbow once in silent thanks, slipped into a clean starched white smock, pinned her hair tighter at the nape, and walked straight down the ground-floor corridor into Majewski's cramped, book-lined office without knocking softly.

Fat SS-Sturmbannführer Doktor Krantz stood warming his broad backside against the lukewarm cast-iron radiator while pale, bespectacled *Amtsarzt* Müller ran a ruler down Pavilion Three's linen-bound pharmacy ledger across Majewski's blotter. Standing just inside the window embrasure sorting pathology slips—though his ears strained visibly toward every syllable—hovered Dr. Roman Karski.

"Good morning, Pan Profesor. Gentlemen," Sylwia said in cool, brisk Berlin German, stripping off her outdoor leather gloves without a trace of haste. "Forgive the mud on my clogs; third-floor tenement stairs on Krochmalna haven't been swept since snow fell, and retained placentas rarely wait for tram schedules. Do you require my keys to the theater cabinet, Herr Amtsarzt?"

Krantz looked her up and down with sour, piggy resentment—still smarting from the October morning she had humiliated his vascular anatomy in front of Reinhardt and Brandt. "Remarkable stamina, Frau Doktor. Yesterday morning clarifying files on Aleja Szucha, yesterday evening attending Oberstleutnant Schoeler in the German quarter, and delivering tenements at dawn."

"Warsaw offers ample practice in stamina, Herr Sturmbannführer," Sylwia replied smoothly, laying her brass key-ring neatly beside Müller's ruler. "Count the ether tins personally if you wish; you will find eleven half-kilo Schering cans, three short of October's quota because Ujazdów requisitioned our November allocation."

Behind his desk, white-bearded Professor Tadeusz Majewski adjusted his gold pince-nez, hiding a tiny, relieved exhale behind dry academic formality. For thirty minutes the old gentleman had risked Pawiak forging her night ward round in ink barely dry; when Müller finally stamped the ledger *Geprüft* with grudging bureaucratic satisfaction and stumped out behind Krantz toward Ward Four, Majewski waited until Karski hurried out after them—ostensibly to unlock the splint room—before rising swiftly to close his oak office door.

Turning back to Sylwia under the tarnished portrait of Józef Piłsudski hidden behind a reversible anatomical chart of the lymphatic system, fifty-eight-year-old Tadeusz Majewski looked suddenly every day of seventy.

"Sit down for two minutes, child, before you fall down," the old surgeon said quietly in Polish, pouring half a glass of lukewarm barley tea from his thermos. He had never called her *child* since thirty-six when she passed her surgical boards first in her class; hearing it now tightened Sylwia's throat unexpectedly. "Halina washed the blood out of your spare stockings yesterday afternoon—no, don't stiffen, Władek saw the Referat Four car take you at nine-thirty, and I know what red concrete looks like on wool knees from thirty-nine."

Sylwia wrapped both chilled hands around the chipped glass without drinking. "Nobody in this hospital was named down there, Pan Profesor. The boy they showed me died clean."

Majewski bowed his white head briefly, making the barest thumb-cross over his waistcoat button. "Eternal rest to him. Listen to me carefully, Sylwia—I ask nothing about how Oberstleutnant Schoeler pulled you out of that building alive, nor why his adjutant's courier left a Wehrmacht disinfection circular on our pharmacy clipboard ten minutes ago."

Sylwia's eyes sharpened instantly. *Schedule Alpha—Brandt had already planted the hospital canary sheet!*

"Only watch your back around Roman," Majewski murmured heavily, glancing toward the frosted glass panel where Karski's shadow had lingered two minutes earlier. "Yesterday morning, ten minutes after Krauss drove you away toward Aleja Szucha, I walked into the doctors' lavatory unannounced. Roman was washing out a fresh Pravaz syringe—and sitting open on the porcelain cistern beside his coat were two full factory-sealed twenty-milligram cartons of German Bayer morphine. Not black-market Russian ampoules; clean Wehrmacht-grade Bayer stock we haven't seen inside Polish pharmacies since forty."

Cold confirmation settled like lead shot into Sylwia's stomach. "Say nothing to him, Tadeusz. Leave that Wehrmacht disinfection circular right where Brandt's courier laid it on the pharmacy clipboard—let Roman read every line if he reaches for it. And keep the cellar hatch double-bolted until Sunday."

"God keep your hands steady, Sylwia," old Majewski whispered, opening the door back onto the noisy ward corridor. "I don't know which wire you are walking anymore, only that half this pavilion hangs from your balance."

***

Ludwig had underestimated *Oddział II*'s speed by forty-eight hours; they didn't wait until Friday.

At four-fifteen that very Wednesday afternoon, as purple winter twilight dropped like an iron shutter over snow-slushed streets, Halina touched Sylwia's sleeve outside Pavilion Three's plaster room and murmured without moving her lips: *"Old aunt on Żurawia has pleurisy—Number Eighteen, courtyard photographer's attic, four-thirty sharp."*

Number Eighteen Żurawia was an emergency cut-out flat used only when sector security had blown a fuse.

Walking fast through biting wind with wet snow soaking through the worn leather soles of her shoes, Sylwia climbed four dark flights of back service stairs smelling of developer hypo and boiled cabbage, gave the November parity knock—*three, one, two*—and stepped into a cramped, black-curtained portrait studio lit by a single amber darkroom safelight.

Marek Zawadzki stood leaning against a dry-plate press in his wet sheepskin jacket, unshaven, dark eyes hollowed and burning. Seated at the scarred retouching table cleaning fingernails with a bone penknife sat a man Sylwia had met only once before, in forty-two when taking her officer's oath in *Konspiracja*: fifty years old, iron-grey crewcut, missing the little finger of his left hand from Pilsudski's First Brigade in nineteen-fifteen, wearing the threadbare black cassock of a suburban parish priest.

Major *Rawicz*—head of *Oddział II* Counter-Intelligence for Warsaw Left Bank.

Nobody offered Sylwia a chair.

"Close the inner baize curtain, Kora," Rawicz said in a dry, rustling voice like dead beech leaves. "At nine-thirty-five yesterday morning you entered Number Twenty-Five Aleja Szucha in a Referat Four staff car. At ten-fifty-two—seventy-seven minutes later—you exited the front entrance unhandcuffed beside Oberstleutnant Ludwig Schoeler, entered his Wehrmacht vehicle, drove to Number Twelve Aleja Róż in the German quarter, and remained inside his private quarters until zero-five-forty-eight this morning. Meanwhile, Courier *Wróbel*—Tomek Kwiatkowski—lies in the municipal morgue wagon with four fingernails missing and his skull split open."

Over by the dry-plate press, Marek did not look at the floor; his dark gaze bored straight into Sylwia's face—searching, agonized, silently begging her to hand Rawicz steel before his own jaw clenched shut at something indefinable he caught around her storm-grey eyes.

Sylwia unbuttoned her wet collar half an inch—letting Rawicz see her throat calm, pulse unhurried—and stood straight between both men.

"Tomek saved all nineteen addresses in this sector," Sylwia said clearly, evenly. "Reinhardt called me in without an arrest warrant on pharmacy pretexts—because Reinhardt wired Berlin last week and obtained photostats proving Ludwig Schoeler and I graduated from the same Berlin secondary school in nineteen-thirty."

Marek jerked upright off the press. "You *what*?! Christ Almighty, Sylwia, three weeks Schoeler's been in your hands and you never reported pre-war personal contact?!"

"Because reporting schoolyard rivalry from thirteen years ago on day one would have pulled me off Pavilion Three inside ten minutes and put an untrained stranger beside his bed!" Sylwia snapped back without yielding half an inch to either man. "Ask yourself whether my keeping silent worked or failed, Grot! When Reinhardt laid that nineteen-thirty graduation photograph on his desk yesterday morning, I fed him three years of genuine pre-war venom between the Kotarski and Schoeler families—verifiable right up to SS-Obersturmbannführer Horst Schoeler in Berlin! Then Reinhardt dragged me down into Cell Four to test my pulse over Tomek."

Her voice didn't falter, though her right hand closed briefly inside her coat pocket. "Tomek recognized me instantly, spat blood onto my hospital coat calling me a German whore in front of two bilingual guards, and threw himself headfirst onto a cast-iron steam flange before they could put him back on the magneto. Five minutes later Schoeler marched in waving a Mauerwald counter-espionage warrant from Generalmajor Gehlen simply to keep Reinhardt from poaching an OKH-commandeered asset—and tore two chest stitches open doing it, which is why I spent curfew night sewing his collarbone back together instead of letting him bleed out and bringing German cordons down on Lindleya!"

Rawicz watched her across the amber safelight without blinking for ten seconds. Fifty years of reading traitors, cowards, and heroes had honed the old legionary's nose to razor sharpness: traitors babbled excuses and sweated around the upper lip; this tall, starving surgeon spoke with cold fury and grief packed under six inches of surgical ice.

Slowly, Rawicz folded his bone penknife shut with a soft *click*.

"Courier *Wróbel* receives the Cross of Valor posthumously in London's next dispatch," Rawicz said quietly. "As for your failure to report pre-war acquaintance with the target, Pani Doktor—under field regulations I ought to suspend your clearance today. Fortunately for you, *Kronos* cannot afford luxuries."

Reaching into a black leather breviary pouch beside his elbow, Rawicz laid two items on the retouching glass under the amber bulb.

The first looked completely innocent: a standard ten-centimeter nickel-plated **Henning ethyl-chloride local-anesthesia spray cylinder** with a brass spring nozzle, identical to three cylinders rattling inside Sylwia's surgical bag every day. Twisting the bottom seam counterclockwise four turns, Rawicz slid out of a lead-lined inner sleeve a gleaming, cigarette-lighter-sized subminiature **Riga Minox** camera loaded with fifty-exposure nine-point-five-millimeter microfilm.

The second item was a small wooden matchbox packed with cotton wool cradling two tiny, unlabelled clear glass ampoules holding two milliliters each of colorless fluid.

"Technical Research Bureau," Rawicz explained briskly. "Crystalline aconitine combined with strophanthin-G in isotonic saline. Tasteless in dark liquid—coffee, cognac, soup—or indistinguishable inside an intravenous syringe. Oral ingestion produces refractory ventricular fibrillation forty to ninety minutes later, mimicking delayed post-traumatic coronary thrombosis or vagal arrest; intravenous acts within four minutes."

Marek stepped forward into the amber light, face grim as tombstone granite. "Red Army spearheads took Kiev last week and broke west toward Zhitomir, Sylwia. OKH is shifting four panzer and artillery divisions east through the Warsaw rail hub between December first and tenth. London needs Schoeler's December transport schedule from that villa before Saturday night—and once we have it, Schoeler cannot remain alive to trace *Kronos*."

Sylwia looked down at the nickel ethyl-chloride tube and the two colorless poison vials gleaming dull orange under the darkroom bulb.

*Three days,* echoed Ludwig's quiet voice from five-thirty this morning before the mirror. *Give me seventy-two hours to lay salted bait on three plates.*

If she refused to put both items into her bag right now, she would never walk down those four flights of stairs on Żurawia alive; Rawicz had two armed liquidation boys sitting in the kitchen behind the baize curtain for anyone whose nerve broke under suspicion.

Without a single tremor, Sylwia unscrewed her own genuine ethyl-chloride cylinder from her satchel, swapped Rawicz's Minox-loaded twin into the elastic loop beside her scalpels, tucked the matchbox of aconitine-strophanthin neatly into her watch-pocket, and snapped the brass frame shut.

"Saturday night at twenty hundred hours," Rawicz said, rising in his black priest's cassock. "Drop the exposed Minox cassette inside the hollow plaster base of Saint Anthony's candle-stand at All Saints' Church on Plac Grzybowski. By Sunday noon we expect Berlin radio to announce Oberstleutnant Schoeler's regrettable relapse."

Halfway down the dark, freezing stairwell, boots hurried down behind her and Marek caught her elbow on the second-floor landing out of earshot of the attic door.

In the dim grey light filtering through cracked stairwell glass, Marek's lean, powder-scarred face looked ravaged by four years of underground war and something much more personal. For three seconds he searched her storm-grey eyes in silence.

"Back in forty on Puławska," Marek whispered roughly, "when the typhoid fever broke and you still shivered for three nights, I learned every shadow on your face, Sylwia. Don't lie to *me*—not down here. Did he force himself on you inside Aleja Róż last night to buy your pass out of Szucha?"

A knife twisted silently inside Sylwia's chest—sharp, unbearable guilt not for loving where no Pole was permitted to look, but because Marek Zawadzki was a brave, decent man slowly turning to iron in a burning house, ready to blow Ludwig's brains out tomorrow in honorable defense of *her* honor if she nodded once.

"Nobody forces me, Marek," she said gently, pulling her elbow cleanly out of his grip. "Not Reinhardt, and not Ludwig Schoeler. Go watch your rail lines and stay out of street *łapanki*; we've buried enough boys this month."

Walking alone southward through swirling snow toward the German district barrier at Plac Trzech Krzyży for her six-o'clock evening dressing appointment at Number Twelve Aleja Róż, carrying Polish aconitine poison in her pocket toward a man who had carried her mother's silver clasp over his heart for thirteen years, Sylwia laughed once under her breath into the freezing wind—a sound without one drop of mirth in it.

*Check to both kings,* she thought bleakly as the Wehrmacht sentry lifted the striped barrier pole to salute her red-sealed pass. *Let's see whose trap springs first.*
