# CHAPTER TWELVE

## Aleja Róż

**Warsaw — December 3, 1943**

Snow lay two feet deep under the black iron streetlamps of Aleja Róż, turning the *Nur für Deutsche* quarter around Ujazdów Park into a muffled Christmas-card engraving patrolled by men with machine pistols.

Inside Number Twelve, the house telephone on the second-floor study wall buzzed twice at six-twenty on Friday evening, just as Sylwia finished swabbing clean pink granulation tissue below Ludwig's left collarbone. Three weeks and two days after October nineteenth, fibrous callus had finally bridged the resected gap of his clavicle firmly enough that indoors he wore no sling at all, moving his left forearm freely from the elbow so long as he didn't lift heavy weight above shoulder level.

Ludwig picked up the Bakelite receiver, listened five seconds to Hauptmann Brandt's clipped voice downstairs, and glanced at his watch. "Put Mauerwald on the scrambler in the cellar booth, Viktor; keep the line open." Setting the receiver back on its cradle, he reached for his unbuttoned field-grey tunic without bothering to hook the collar. "Twelve minutes, Frau Doktor—signal relay from East Prussia drops out at half past if I don't take it live. Leave the fresh adhesive laid out; I'll let you finish strapping when I come up."

Door latch clicked. Measured boot-treads descended the curving oak stairs, followed faintly ten seconds later by the thud of the padded basement cipher-room door closing two floors below.

Silence settled thick and warm across the second-floor study.

Sylwia stood motionless beside her open medical bag, listening to anthracite pop softly behind the iron hearth grille and her own blood beat cleanly behind her ears.

Tomorrow night at eight o'clock, Major Rawicz's courier would reach inside the hollow plaster pedestal of Saint Anthony's candle-stand at All Saints' Church on Plac Grzybowski. If that hollow plaster held no exposed Minox microfilm cassette—or if Sunday passed with Oberstleutnant Schoeler striding briskly down Aleje Ujazdowskie—Monday morning brought an *Oddział II* surveillance review she would not walk away from twice.

Slowly, Sylwia turned her head toward the broad mahogany desk six paces away.

Sitting squarely beside the green-shaded brass student lamp rested Ludwig's black calfskin Generalstab dispatch case, brass hasp clicked shut. Behind the cut-crystal inkwell, half-hidden beneath a blotter corner yet catching one tiny glint of yellow lamplight, lay the small flat warded brass spare key.

*Too visible?* whispered a sudden prickle between her shoulder blades. No—for three evenings running she had noticed Brandt toss spare keys into that exact porcelain pen-dish while sorting dispatches. Still, her surgeon's hands slipped first into fresh dry powdered Para-rubber gloves from her satchel so neither brass nor paper would hold skin oils.

Crossing noiselessly to the desk, she lifted the brass key, slid it into the calfskin lock—*snick*—and raised the leather flap.

Right beneath a blank Wehrmacht cipher pad lay two crisp carbon flimsies stapled together under red block capitals:

**GEHEIME KOMMANDOSACHE — TRANSPORTKOMMANDANTUR WARSCHAU**
*Sonderfahrplan Dezember I: Verlegung Pz.-Ersatz-Abt. 17 & Schwere Art.-Abt. 408 (Richtung Orscha–Witebsk)*

Her heart jumped once. Exactly what Marek and Rawicz had begged heaven for: train numbers, locomotive marshaling sidings at Praga, departure hours between December fourth and ninth!

Working swiftly against the gilt mantel clock—nine minutes left—Sylwia reached back to her satchel, unscrewed the bottom seam of Rawicz's hollow Henning ethyl-chloride spray cylinder four turns counterclockwise, and slid the gleaming, palm-sized Latvian Riga Minox camera out of its lead sleeve. Pulling the telescopic aluminum body open half an inch to cock the shutter and advance Frame One of the nine-point-five-millimeter film, she turned the small distance wheel on top to thirty centimeters, switched the green desk lamp two inches closer over the flimsy, raised the tiny rectangular viewfinder to her eye—

And froze dead with her right forefinger half-depressed on the shutter button.

Through the crystal-clear Minox framing window, two details on the lower half of Page One leaped straight up into her cortex like red flares fired over snow.

First, Line Four of the timetable read:

*Zug Nr. 4419 (32 R-Wagen, Pz.Kpfw. V 'Panther', Gesamtlast 1.680 t) — Durchfahrt Siedlce Ost über Liwiec-Brücke Joch IV am 4.XII um 23.10 Uhr.*

*Thirty-two flatcars carrying forty-five-ton Panther tanks crossing Liwiec River Bridge Span Four east of Siedlce tomorrow night.*

Fourteen days ago, down in Ward Six of Infant Jesus Hospital under armed *Bahnschutz* guard, Sylwia had spent two hours debriding third-degree steam burns across the chest and forearms of a Polish *Ostbahn* locomotive fireman scalded half to death when a Kedyw sapper charge blew **Bridge Span Four** east of Siedlce clean off its masonry piers into the frozen Liwiec River! Every railwayman in Warsaw knew German pioneer engineers were still routing light supply trains over a temporary pine-trestle shoofly rated for fifteen metric tons per axle—drive forty-five-ton Panther tanks onto that timber bypass and the whole train plunged straight through the ice!

Slowly lowering the Minox camera from her eye, Sylwia bent six inches closer under the green lamp to inspect the bottom right corner of the carbon flimsy, right beside the column addition of gross tonnage.

There, penciled so lightly in hard grey graphite that nine hundred ninety-nine eyes out of a thousand would have dismissed it for a stray flyspeck on rough wartime paper, sat a tiny, two-millimeter Greek minuscule *gamma* (γ) with its lower loop hooked sharply backward to the left.

Sixteen years dropped away inside half a heartbeat.

Chalk dust floating in afternoon sunbeams above the Spree at Reichstagsufer Six; Herr Doktor Glass tapping his vulcanite jaw while seventeen-year-old Ludwig Schoeler stood beside the blackboard proving spherical trigonometry by *reductio ad absurdum*—always, out of unconscious private habit whenever constructing a false premise designed to collapse at the final line, marking the assumed false equation in the lower right corner of his slate with a tiny backward-hooked Greek *gamma*.

*Schedule Gamma.*

*Three plates,* Ludwig had told her quietly before the mirror at five-thirty Wednesday morning. *Give me seventy-two hours to lay salted bait on three plates.*

Two plates for the hospital and the street.

And a third plate—locked right inside his personal calfskin dispatch case with the brass key glinting coyly behind the inkwell—laid exclusively for **Sylwia Kotarska**.

For five full seconds, standing alone in the quiet firelit study in powdered surgical gloves, Sylwia felt rage so cold, pure, and incandescent rise up from the soles of her feet that her fingertips actually steadied to granite. After Cell Four—after pulling her mother's silver leaf-clasp out of his breast pocket—after holding her naked against his ribs while snow fell and swearing total war on Reinhardt together—the arrogant, incorrigible Prussian bastard had *still* laid a canary trap in front of her feet to see whether she would bite!

And half a heartbeat behind that fury came a fierce, wicked, exhilarating rush of pure intellectual triumph that no drug on earth had ever matched: *He tested me—and I caught him cold inside ninety seconds.*

Downstairs on the ground floor, a distant heavy door thudded shut. Three minutes before his boot reached the landing.

Sylwia did not lock the flimsy back inside the briefcase. She did not hide the Minox camera back inside the ethyl-chloride tube. Stripping off her rubber gloves and tossing them neatly back into her satchel, she left **Schedule Gamma** lying wide open in the bright pool of the green desk lamp, laid the gleaming naked **Riga Minox** directly across the backward Greek *gamma* in the lower margin, reached into her watch-pocket for Rawicz's wooden matchbox, slid it halfway open so both colorless two-milliliter glass ampoules of **aconitine-strophanthin** glinted on white cotton beside his crystal inkwell, walked calmly over to the roaring anthracite hearth, and stood facing the door with her arms folded loosely beneath her breasts.

Measured footsteps climbed the oak stairs. The brass handle turned; Ludwig stepped inside, closing the heavy double doors and turning the key automatically behind his back before looking toward the dressing chair—

And stopping dead on the edge of the Tabriz carpet.

In a fifth of a second his Generalstab eyes swept the illuminated mahogany desk: unlocked calfskin case, Schedule Gamma spread flat under the lamp, a subminiature Latvian spy camera resting squarely on the corner margin, and two unlabeled crystal poison ampoules gleaming in a matchbox.

Slowly, Ludwig raised his slate-blue eyes across twelve feet of firelight to Sylwia.

"Bridge Span Four over the Liwiec east of Siedlce dropped into the river on November nineteenth, Herr Oberstleutnant," Sylwia said in cool, crystalline Berlin German that could have sliced diamonds. "Even pioneer timber trestles sink under forty-five-ton Panther tanks—I skin-grafted the scalded *Ostbahn* fireman myself two weeks ago. And if you intend laying *reductio ad absurdum* canary traps for me like a seventeen-year-old boy in Doktor Glass's fourth-period study hall, Ludwig, at least take an India-rubber eraser to that backward Greek *gamma* in the bottom corner before leaving the key behind the inkpot."

Dead silence hung in the warm room. Coal settled with a tiny red shower of sparks behind the grate.

For three seconds Ludwig stood utterly motionless—taking the hit squarely between the eyes without blinking, watching the storm-grey lightning blazing across the rug at him—and then, slowly, unexpectedly, the hard tension bracketing his mouth broke into a low, breathless, astonished laugh.

Walking straight to the desk, he picked up Schedule Gamma, glanced once at the microscopic graphite hook in the corner he hadn't consciously realized his own hand still drew after fifteen years, shook his head in sheer, helpless wonder, and tossed the fake timetable straight onto the glowing anthracite coals, where it curled instantly into bright orange flame.

"God in heaven above," Ludwig murmured, turning back to look at her as paper burned golden behind her hip. "Give me three divisions with eyes like yours and I'd be in Moscow by Tuesday. Nobody else in Europe—not Gehlen, not Canaris, certainly not Reinhardt—would have spotted that margin tick in a hundred years."

"Don't think flattering my eyesight saves your skin, Schoeler!" Sylwia snapped, unfolding her arms and stepping sharply forward, genuine hurt and fury sparking hot beneath her triumph. "After Tuesday night in this bed—after telling me about Smolensk and Tresckow—you *still* put poisoned paper under my nose with a spare key sticking out like cheese in a mousetrap?!"

"Look at what is sitting beside my inkwell right now before you take my head off, Sylwia!" Ludwig countered sharply, pointing one lean finger at the Riga Minox and the open matchbox of clear glass vials. "Did I misread your *Oddział II* handlers by five minutes, or did they haul you into a darkroom within twenty-four hours of leaving my gate and order you to photograph my safe and poison my coffee by Sunday?!"

Her angry retort died halfway up her throat, because both pieces of lethal hardware sat shining damningly on polished mahogany six feet away.

"I didn't test *your* loyalty, you maddening woman," Ludwig said more quietly, crossing the carpet until two paces separated them. "I knew they would put a gun to your back before Friday! Had they stood over you or forced you to shoot that roll blind to save your own neck from an underground court-martial, London would have transmitted the Siedlce coordinate by Monday—giving me twelve hours' advance warning to pull you out of Warsaw before Rawicz or Reinhardt squeezed you dry! Instead..."

His slate-blue eyes softened into something fierce, marveling, and wholly unguarded as he looked from the unexposed Minox back to her face.

"Instead you caught the bridge error in ninety seconds, spotted a schoolboy pencil habit from nineteen-twenty-eight, and laid both their camera and their execution ampoules openly on my desk." Stepping past her to pick up the matchbox carefully, Ludwig sniffed the sealed glass tip—odorless—and raised one brow. "Cyanide or digitalis?"

"Crystalline aconitine with strophanthin-G," Sylwia answered grimly. "Rawicz wants the exposed Minox cassette inside Saint Anthony's candle-stand at All Saints' Church tomorrow night at eight, and Berlin radio announcing your coronary arrest by Sunday noon."

Ludwig closed the matchbox, walked behind the tall oil landscape on the side wall, spun a hidden brass combination dial four times, swung open a recessed steel wall-safe—the *real* safe neither Brandt's visitors nor casual burglars knew existed—tossed the poison matchbox inside, and drew out a folded linen-backed military map overlay marked in blue grease-pencil alongside three genuine teleprinter intercepts from SS and Police Leader Cracow.

Laying them flat under the green desk lamp instead of the burned canary sheet, Ludwig picked up the Riga Minox himself, verified the thirty-centimeter focus chain, and handed the tiny silver camera straight back into Sylwia's palm.

"Shoot twelve frames of these," he said crisply. "Genuine *Ordnungspolizei* and *SS-Polizei-Regiment 22* anti-partisan sweep coordinates scheduled between December sixth and twelfth through the Kampinos and Mińsk Mazowiecki forests, plus two real ammunition train numbers out of Brest that carry zero Smolensk Generalstab watermarks. Your people save forty forest fighters from encirclement, London verifies the Brest trains by Tuesday morning, and Major Rawicz kisses both your hands."

Sylwia stared down at the genuine blue-penciled SS sweep map, throat tightening unexpectedly at what he was handing her without hesitation—German police operational secrets carrying ten automatic death sentences for him if traced—before checking the tactical flaw instantly: "And Sunday noon, when you aren't dead of ventricular fibrillation?"

"Tell Rawicz through your nurse tomorrow night that my adjutant tastes every drop of food and locks the medicine chest since Tuesday's clash with Reinhardt, so oral dosing failed and you need a contact toxin or seven days until Brandt goes to Königsberg," Ludwig answered without missing half a beat. "Seven days is all we need—because two hours ago, before you walked in, Reinhardt's plainclothes street shadow inquired casually at Praga yardmaster's office about **Schedule Alpha**."

Sylwia's eyes widened sharply. "*Schedule Alpha*? The hospital disinfection carbon?!"

"Left on Pavilion Three's administrative clipboard Wednesday noon," Ludwig nodded, eyes glinting cold and victorious. "*Jaskółka* is definitely inside Infant Jesus Hospital, Sylwia—and inside forty-eight hours Brandt's micro-dye on the pharmacy telephone coin-box tells us whether Roman Karski's thumbs glow ultraviolet."

Taking the tiny Minox in steady hands, Sylwia clicked cleanly through fourteen crisp exposures under the green desk lamp—*click-slide, click-slide*—slid the camera back inside its lead-lined ethyl-chloride sleeve, screwed the brass nozzle tight, and set it inside her black bag.

Then she straightened beside the desk, turned slowly toward Ludwig in the warm orange glow of the anthracite fire, and felt every coiled spring of terror, relief, intellectual intoxication, and delayed fury vibrate taut as violin gut down to the tips of her bare fingers.

"Turn around," she said coolly, picking up three-inch adhesive strapping scissors. "Let me finish taping your collarbone before I decide whether to forgive that backward *gamma*."

Ludwig didn't turn around.

Reaching out with both hands—left hand moving smoothly alongside his right now at waist height—he took the steel scissors cleanly out of her fingers, laid them flat onto the mahogany desk behind her without taking his eyes off hers, and backed her two slow, deliberate steps until her shoulder blades met the leather-bound spines of the floor-to-ceiling bookcase beside the hearth.

"Forgive?" Ludwig murmured, planting his right forearm on the shelf beside her temple and resting his left hand lightly against her waist—not pinning her frantic wrists like All Saints' Night in Pavilion Three, something infinitely more dangerous now because zero illusion stood left between them. "Nobody between the Rhine and the Vistula ever survived forgiving the other where you and I are concerned, Kotarska. Admit you loved catching that *gamma* more than breathing."

"Go to the devil, Schoeler," she breathed—and couldn't stop the fierce, wicked curve lifting one corner of her mouth half a second before his mouth came down slow, hot, and claiming onto hers.

No frantic tearing of blood-soaked linen tonight. Tonight they owned fifty quiet minutes behind locked oak while snow muffled Warsaw two feet deep outside, and Ludwig took his time as though stripping away every defense she still kept buttoned to her throat were a campaign he had planned since nineteen-twenty-eight.

Kissing her slowly, deeply against the leather book-spines—tongue stroking unhurriedly against hers until her knees softened inside wool stockings and her hands slid up inside his unfastened field-grey tunic to push the heavy crimson-tabbed wool off both his shoulders onto the carpet—he reached behind her nape with his steady right hand and pulled out her heavy steel hairpins one by one, letting them fall *tink... tink... tink* onto the oak border beside the rug until dark chestnut waves spilled heavy and warm down past her shoulder blades.

"Unbutton your blouse," Ludwig whispered against the sensitive hollow below her ear, teeth grazing her earlobe lightly enough to raise gooseflesh straight down both arms. "Slowly. Let me watch those famous steady hands."

Heat flushed crimson from Sylwia's throat down between her breasts. Under any other man's eyes on earth she would have laughed in his face at an order; under Ludwig's dark-slate, intensely focused gaze six inches away, undoing her own white pearl buttons one by one felt shamelessly, intoxicatingly erotic—watching his nostrils flare slightly and his breath roughen as dark rose-tipped breasts swelled bare out of parted white cotton chemise ribbons.

Before her fingers reached her skirt waist, Ludwig knelt smoothly on both knees on the thick Persian wool rug before the open coal fire, unfastened her dark skirt so it dropped around her ankles, rolled down wool stockings and garter belt together with both warm hands gliding slow up bare calves and inner thighs, and hooked two thumbs inside the narrow elastic of her plain white batiste knickers to draw them off her bare feet.

Standing completely naked before the glowing orange grate while he remained dressed only in unbuttoned white linen shirt hanging loose around lean General Staff riding breeches, Sylwia tangled her fingers lightly in his cropped tarnished-gold hair to pull him up—only for Ludwig to catch both her wrists gently, kiss the pale transfusion scar inside her left elbow, and turn her smoothly half around to face the wide velvet-upholstered Biedermeier chaise-longue set diagonally before the fire.

"Hands on the velvet bolster, *Frau Doktor*," he murmured low against her bare spine, kissing slowly down one vertebra after another from her seventh cervical down to the twin dimples above her buttocks while goosebumps raced wildly across her thighs. "Every hour of your life you carry twenty people's lives on that straight spine of yours. For thirty minutes tonight, put it down."

A dark, liquid shiver broke clean through Sylwia's belly. Bending forward to brace both forearms across the high scrolled velvet arm of the chaise—arched back glowing warm apricot in the anthracite firelight, heavy dark hair spilling across ruby plush, long pale legs parted slightly on the soft carpet—she gasped sharply as Ludwig knelt directly behind her, spread her bare thighs wider with two firm scarred hands, and buried his hot mouth squarely between her legs from behind.

The angle left nothing shielded and nothing polite: flat wet strokes of his tongue dragging all the way forward from drenched entrance to throbbing clitoris while his thumbs held the round curves of her buttocks parted to the fire-heat, nose and rough golden chin-stubble pressing deep into slick, swollen flesh until Sylwia bit straight into the ruby velvet cushion to keep from crying out loud enough to carry down two floors.

"Please—Ludwig—" Broken German, then involuntary Polish—*"Boże, teraz—"*—slipped raggedly past her teeth as his right middle and ring fingers slid deep inside her from behind, curling downward in slow, relentless hooks while he stood up smoothly behind her, unbuttoning his breeches in two jerks and pressing his bare, fever-hot chest lightly down along her arched naked spine so his healing left collarbone rested safe in air while his lips found the racing pulse at the side of her neck.

Notching the broad, slick head of his cock against her dripping entrance from behind, Ludwig slid his right palm flat across her bare lower belly to pull her hips flush back against his groin—and drove forward in one slow, thick, bottomless stroke that filled her completely to the cervix.

Sylwia's fingers clawed white-knuckled into velvet upholstery, eyes rolling half-shut in pure, shameless animal bliss as every inch inside her stretched tight around burning iron.

"Still think you can hide one thought from me, Sylwia?" Ludwig rasped hotly into her ear, teeth nipping the bare curve of her shoulder as he began moving—slow, heavy, deliberate strokes at first, pulling almost all the way out into cool air so she whimpered involuntarily and pushed back greedily to recapture him before slamming his hips solidly forward against her bare buttocks with a soft, rhythmic *slap* of sweat-warmed skin that echoed the crackle of the fire.

Soon slow control burned clean away in both of them: his strong right hand slid up from her belly to cup one bare swinging breast rough and full before dropping back down between her parted thighs to rub fast, slick, merciless circles directly over her clitoris in time with the hard, driving piston-thrusts of his hips from behind, his healthy left hand gripping her hipbone to steady the angle while every breath ragged hot against her shoulder blades.

"Come for me," he breathed harshly against her wet neck, feeling the unmistakable velvet vise inside her begin fluttering wildly around his shaft—"Don't hold back—let go, Sylwia—"

Everything inside her shattered outward into white-hot shards at once—knees buckling onto the padded seat edge of the chaise if his arm around her waist hadn't held her pinned upright against him, inner walls clamping down in violent, rhythmic convulsions that dragged a raw, unguarded groan straight out of Ludwig's throat as he buried himself balls-deep one final shuddering stroke and poured scalding heat deep inside her body, collapsing gently sideways with her afterward down onto the wide velvet chaise before the fire without slipping free for long, quiet minutes while both their hearts thundered against sweat-damp skin.

Pulling a folded grey wool carriage blanket off the chaise foot over both their naked bodies as the coal fire sank to deep cherry red, Ludwig lay on his right side behind her, cradling her spine tight against his chest with her head pillowed on his bicep while his right fingertips traced idle, absent patterns just below her bare left breast where her own heartbeat gradually slowed.

Outside in the frozen garden, eight-o'clock curfew bells tolled faintly from Saint Alexander's across white rooftops—meaning tonight she had a fresh twenty-four-hour *Nachtdienst* pass Brandt had stamped so no street patrol could touch her at dawn.

Half-drifting in rare, miraculous warmth she hadn't felt in four starving winters, Sylwia thought he had fallen asleep behind her—until she felt his lips brush her bare temple and speak three slow, careful, slightly stiff-consonanted words she had never once in sixteen years heard leave Ludwig Schoeler's Prussian mouth:

*"Nie bój się, Sylwia."*

*Do not be afraid, Sylwia.*

Her breath caught clean in the dark. Turning her head half around on his arm to stare at him in the dim red glow, she saw a faint, almost sheepish crooked line at the corner of his mouth.

"Where on earth did you learn Polish?" she whispered in disbelief.

"Bought a pocket grammar off Brandt's station newsstand last week," Ludwig murmured into her hair, tightening his arm around her bare waist and closing his eyes. "Terrible spelling—four consonants where civilized nations use one—just in case I ever have to argue with you after Berlin burns down. Go to sleep, *Frau Doktor*. Tomorrow the wolves run."

# CHAPTER THIRTEEN

## The Canary Trap

**Warsaw — Mid-December 1943**

Under ordinary room light, anthracene-sulfate compounded with finely milled quinine bisulfate looked no different from dry radiator dust on painted iron.

At eleven o'clock on the morning of December eleventh, inside the pitch-black radiological fluoroscopy cabinet off Pavilion Three's ground-floor corridor, Sylwia flipped the brass toggle switch of the Hanau quartz mercury-vapor Wood's lamp. Normally the deep violet-black beam served to screen ragged children from Wola orphanages for *Microsporum* scalp ringworm, which fluoresced bright apple-green in the dark.

"Hold the lower corner of the barium plate steady for me a second, Roman, while I center the lead diaphragm," Sylwia asked casually over her shoulder as Dr. Roman Karski stepped behind the black oilcloth curtain carrying Ward Four's morning chest films.

"Make it quick, Pani Doktor, admissions is backed up to the stairs," Karski muttered irritably. Since Tuesday his skin had taken on a waxy, greenish-tallow sheen; twice during yesterday's appendectomy his hand had jerked badly enough on the retractor to bruise rectus muscle, and he kept wiping cold sweat off his upper lip with a crumpled handkerchief. Reaching across the lead-glass frame in the pitch dark to steady the cassette corner for her, he rested his bare right thumb and index finger squarely inside the invisible ultraviolet cone of the Wood's filter.

Sylwia did not move a muscle in the darkness.

Glowing stark, unmistakable, ghostly ice-blue along the whorls of Roman Karski's right thumbpad, index fingertip, and the inner crease of his palm—shaped precisely like the brass receiver hook of the courtyard public payphone and the corner clip of Tuesday's salted Wehrmacht rail-disinfection carbon in the pharmacy safe—shone the quinine-anthracene trace Hauptmann Brandt had mixed for her forty-eight hours ago.

*Roman.*

Even expecting it since last week, seeing that electric blue glow on the hand of a Polish physician who had shared bread crusts and carried wounded children down burning stairs beside her in September thirty-nine turned Sylwia's stomach briefly to lead.

"Thank you, Roman," she said evenly, snapping off the violet Wood's lamp before his pinprick eyes could look down and wonder why his own fingertips shone like corpse-candles. Flipping on the red darkroom bulb instead, she took the cassette calmly from his hands. "Go take twenty minutes for hot tea in the doctors' room; you look chilled through."

Karski didn't want tea, and both of them knew it. Thirty minutes earlier, huddled behind a frozen locomotive water-tower near Dworzec Gdański while December sleet blew sideways off the Vistula, Karski had endured the worst ten minutes of his thirty-five years across an upturned crate from SS-Sturmbannführer Otto Reinhardt.

Reinhardt had not offered Dutch cocoa or polite Bavarian pear drops today.

*"Polizei-Regiment Twenty-Two encircled three empty charcoal camps in Kampinos Forest on Wednesday morning, Doktor Karski,"* Reinhardt had said softly, cold wind whistling through bare telegraph wires overhead while Karski shivered uncontrollably inside his thin overcoat. *"Someone in Warsaw handed the bandits our exact sector boundaries forty-eight hours ahead of H-hour. Worse, that Praga rail timetable you telephoned in last week—Schedule Alpha—was a salted canary trap planted by Oberstleutnant Schoeler's adjutant! Had my clerk forwarded it to Berlin unverified, I should look like an amateur!"*

Karski had practically dropped to his knees in the black cinders when Reinhardt opened his gloved hand to show *only one* five-ampoule strip of morphine instead of twenty—and tapped a red-bordered *Überstellungsbefehl* transfer form bearing Estera Goldblum's name.

*"Vittel camp is overcrowded, Doktor. Non-productive Latin American passport holders without high-value sponsors transfer east to Birkenau on December twenty-eighth. Bring me concrete, prosecutable proof of what passes between Frau Doktor Kotarska and Oberstleutnant Schoeler inside Number Twelve Aleja Róż before Christmas Eve—something written, photographed, or overheard—or File Four-Nineteen-B boards the Silesian train."*

Now, hurrying out of Sylwia's fluoroscopy booth toward the locked doctors' lavatory with that lone five-ampoule morphine strip burning against his ribs, Roman Karski didn't feel guilt anymore—only a blind, hunted rat's certainty that either Sylwia Kotarska went down before Christmas, or Estera died in Birkenau gas.

Five hours later, inside the warm study on Aleja Róż while snow rattled dry against the casements, Ludwig listened silently as Sylwia reported the glowing blue thumbprints under the Wood's lamp.

"Clean and certain," Ludwig said, walking to the map table in riding boots and unbuttoned white shirt—four weeks post-ambush now, color back in his lean cheeks, moving with ninety percent of his lethal pre-war grace. "No risk of hanging an innocent clerk. Leave Karski untouched for four more days."

Sylwia frowned sharply from the hearth chair. "Leave a Gestapo informant walking loose through my surgical wards for four days? If he spots one bandage going down to the boiler room—"

"If Karski drops dead or vanishes tonight," Ludwig cut in crisply, holding up two fingers in classic Generalstab sequence, "two things happen immediately: Reinhardt knows Pavilion Three uncovered *Jaskółka* and raids your hospital before breakfast, AND Reinhardt puts a fresh, unknown shadow onto us. Whereas a known, desperate double-agent whose strings *we* see is worth his weight in gold! Give Viktor until Tuesday to pull Referat Four's actual internal Hotel Polski transport manifest through our clerk at Szucha. Once we hold paper truth on Estera Goldblum, we own Roman Karski's soul—and use him to feed Otto Reinhardt whatever poison we choose."

Logically, surgically, he was unassailable—yet before Tuesday arrived, Warsaw intervened with claws of its own.

***

On the night of December fifteenth, gale-force east winds straight off the Russian steppes drove temperatures down to sixteen below zero and buried ulica Lindleya in waist-high snowdrifts.

At ten-twenty PM, two hours past curfew while Sylwia was finishing septic ward rounds in Pavilion Three, three frantic iron clangs echoed up the empty metal laundry chute from the sub-basement—Halina's emergency distress code.

Snatching her surgical roll without breaking into a visible run down the corridor, Sylwia slipped through the ground-floor sluice pantry, unlocked the narrow iron spiral stair behind the linen presses, and plunged down into the sweltering, steam-thick brick labyrinth behind the hospital's roaring typhus-disinfection boilers.

Behind the false stack of soda-ash barrels where young Janek had lain last month—Janek had been safely moved out to a Milanówek convalescent farm in a potato wagon ten days ago—flickered a single shielded barn lantern.

Kneeling on the brick floor in her blue nurse's cotton dress soaked dark crimson from apron to knees was twenty-six-year-old Halina Nowak, pressing a folded boiled towel desperately over the bare, board-hard, distended abdomen of a gaunt nineteen-year-old boy wearing torn, frost-stiffened *Ostbahn* railway-laborer overalls. Old night porter Władek stood grey-faced by the coal chute bolting the outer iron hatch.

"Halina—" Sylwia dropped straight to her knees beside the straw pallet, peeling back the crimson-soaked towel for two seconds beneath the yellow lantern flame.

One glance told the entire catastrophic story. Two inches left of the umbilicus gaped the small, blackened, powder-singed entry hole of a seven-point-nine-two Mauser carbine bullet; lower toward the right iliac crest bulged a boggy blue-black subcutaneous hematoma where the deformed lead slug lay lodged under the fascia without exiting. Worse—escaping on a thin bubble of gas from the entry track came the unmistakable feculent odor of perforated small intestine, and across both starved, shivering flanks bloomed tiny, mulberry-pink petechial spots that did not blanch under pressure.

Gunshot perforation of the ileum *and* Day Four louse-borne epidemic typhus.

"It's Jacek," Halina whispered. For four years Sylwia had watched Halina Nowak hold retractors steady while Luftwaffe five-hundred-kilo bombs blew window frames inward across the street; tonight Halina's chin quivered like a terrified child's as she looked up at Sylwia. "My little brother, Pani Doktor—they shipped his Praga locomotive gang toward Brest on Monday when typhus broke out in the barracks, he jumped off a slow flatcar at Dworzec Wschodni two hours ago when a *Bahnschutz* guard shot at him in the dark, and crawled three miles across the frozen river ice under Poniatowski Bridge to reach me! Save him—for the love of Christ's Mother, Pani Doktor, my mother died in forty-one, he's all I have left on earth!"

On the pallet, nineteen-year-old Jacek Nowak—Halina's dark eyes, cracked bleeding lips crusted with frostbite, pulse hammering at one hundred thirty-six—clutched Sylwia's white sleeve with burning, lice-bitten fingers: *"Don't let them take me back to the boxcars, Pani—"*

Before Sylwia could even call for sterile water, two sharp knocks rattled the upper sluice pipe, and Professor Tadeusz Majewski came hurrying down the spiral iron steps two at a time, spectacles fogged instantaneously by boiler steam, white beard trembling violently against his unbuttoned collar.

"Sylwia! God help us—Władek, did he drop blood in the snow coming through the Nowogrodzka coal lane?!"

Władek blanched under coal dust. "Wind was drifting heavy, Pan Profesor, I swept the gate step with a broom, maybe ten yards out in the alley—"

"Two *Bahnschutz* railway patrol trucks and a *Schutzpolizei* squad just turned off Chałubińskiego following blood spots in the drift with two Alsatian tracking dogs!" Majewski gasped, gripping the iron handrail to stay upright. "They are at the main Lindleya courtyard gate right now demanding to search Pavilion Three and the cellars for an escaped railway saboteur! We can't move him out—every gate is blocked!"

In the suffocating brick vault beneath the hospital, nobody breathed.

Look upward: five feet directly above Jacek Nowak's straw pallet, set flush into the vaulted brick ceiling to give daytime light to the outer laundry passage on the other side of an inch-thick pine plank partition behind the soda-ash barrels, ran a six-foot square grid of thick green glass pavement-blocks forming the floor of the covered ground-floor courtyard arcade.

If an Alsatian tracking dog smelled fresh human blood down the service stairs behind the laundry vats—or heard one groan through those glass ceiling blocks—every soul in this basement went against the Nowy Świat brick wall tomorrow noon, and Reinhardt wouldn't need Karski anymore.

And if Sylwia waited forty minutes to open Jacek Nowak's peritoneum while liquid feces poured out of torn ileum into a typhus-weakened bloodstream, irreversible endotoxic shock killed Halina's brother before midnight anyway.

Inside Sylwia's brain, that absolute, glacial battlefield stillness snapped cleanly into command.

"Tadeusz!" Sylwia ordered swiftly, tearing off her outer ward coat and plunging bare hands and forearms straight into Władek's bucket of hot sublimate solution beside the boiler tap while Halina tore open the emergency tin drum of boiled laparotomy linen they kept permanently hidden inside Dummy Boiler Two. "Run straight up to Maternity Delivery Ward One—Sister Zofia delivered twin placentas twenty minutes ago, the enamel slop-pail hasn't gone to the incinerator yet! Take Władek, dump both fresh placentas and two liters of retroplacental blood right across the top of the cellar coal steps and the outer laundry passage, and pour half a carboy of raw fifty-percent carbolic cresol and dry chloride of lime straight on top of it!"

Old Professor Majewski's eyes widened behind misted lenses—catching the sheer, cunning brilliance of it in half a second.

"Human blood at the top of the stairs explains any yard scent as careless hospital incinerator slops," Sylwia hissed rapidly, shaking stinging sublimate off her bare elbows as Halina snapped dry patched rubber gloves onto her hands, "and raw cresol plus chlorine gas burns an Alsatian dog's olfactory mucosa blind for two hours if it puts its snout within six feet! Tell the *Bahnschutz* sergeant we burn typhus sheets and septic afterbirths down here and invite him warmly to come sniff typhus lice himself if he doubts you! Move—now!"

Majewski and Władek scrambled up the spiral iron steps faster than men twenty years younger.

Down behind the soda-ash partition—a brick niche seven feet long by five feet wide, eighty-eight degrees Fahrenheit from the adjacent boiler flue—Sylwia dropped onto both knees in dirty brick dust beside Jacek's low wooden trestle-pallet. No zinc table down here; surgeons who couldn't cut on their knees in cellars belonged in Zurich.

"Strip his overalls away from xiphoid to pubis—scissors, don't pull cloth over the wound—and paint everything drowned in iodine," Sylwia whispered, voice pitched lower than the hiss of steam in the copper boiler valve four feet away. "You handle the ether cone, Halina—open drop, fast induction so he doesn't cry out during the excitement stage when they enter the courtyard. If boots step onto those glass bricks overhead before he's deep in Plane Three, clamp a folded wet towel over the wire mask even if he turns blue for twenty seconds. Understand?"

Halina wiped her wet cheeks once on her shoulder—gone was the weeping sister; back stood Pavilion Three's finest scrub nurse, jaw set like Mazovian oak.

"Understood, Pani Doktor."

"Hold my wrist, Jacek," Sylwia murmured softly, touching the boy's burning temple for one second as Halina lowered the Schimmelbusch wire cone over his frost-chapped nose and tipped raw ether fast onto eight layers of gauze. "Count backward from twenty in your head for Halina. In thirty minutes your belly won't hurt."

Jacek swallowed hard beneath the choking sweet-arctic fumes, eyes rolling wildly once at the second breath as delirious typhus muscle fought ether—his boots kicked slightly toward the soda barrels; Sylwia threw one knee smoothly across both his shins without bruising bone, holding him silent as stone while Halina poured ether recklessly close to saturation until his pupils centered, eyeballs fixed, and diaphragmatic breathing settled deep and automatic.

Upstairs—muffled through vaulted brick, then abruptly loud and sharp directly overhead—heavy iron courtyard gates banged wide, and frantic, deep-chested Alsatian barking echoed off stone arcades alongside shouted German commands!

*"Such, Prinz! Fass! Da drüben beim Kohlenschacht!"*

*Search, Prince! Over there by the coal shaft!*

Sylwia did not look up at the frosted green glass blocks five feet above her crown. Holding a #10 scalpel steady in her right hand under the turned-down yellow lantern wick, she drew a clean six-inch left paramedian incision from two inches above the umbilicus straight down through rectus sheath and transversalis fascia, nicked the bulging, purple-black peritoneum carefully between two hemostats—a foul hiss of trapped intestinal gas and dark blood-stained fecal fluid welled up into Halina's silent hand-sponge—and slid four gloved fingers gently down into the blazing heat of the boy's abdominal cavity.

Directly above her bent dark head, hobnailed boots stepped squarely onto the thick green glass pavement-blocks of the arcade floor—black boot-soles distorted like giant beetles four feet above the lantern, accompanied by the scrabbling iron claws of a ninety-pound Alsatian straining on a leather choke-chain!

Halina's hand froze over the ether bottle, eyes locked in terror on the black boot-shapes overhead; the lantern flame was shielded underneath a tin stove-hood so zero yellow light leaked upward through frosted glass coated in six inches of packed snow, yet six feet of air and mortared glass was all that stood between two women kneeling in human blood and six Mauser carbines.

From twenty yards away at the top of the outer laundry steps drifted Professor Majewski's indignant, aristocratic Vienna German, ringing out magnificently across the echoing arcade:

*"Aber meine Herren, ich warne Sie ausdrücklich—hier unten liegt die Fleckfieber-Wäscherei und der Verbrennungsschacht für septische Nachgeburten! Władek hat gerade Chlorkalk über einen umgekippten Eimer geschüttet—wenn Ihre Hunde Läuse aus den Fleckfieberdecken fangen, bringen Sie das Fleckfieber morgen früh direkt in Ihre eigene Kaserne!"*

*Fleckfieber!*

No single German word east of the Oder cleared space faster than *typhus*. Every soldier from Brest to Kharkov lived in mortal dread of body lice carrying Rickettsia.

Ten yards down the steps came a sudden, explosive canine yelp followed by furious sneezing and hacking coughs as two Alsatians plunged eager muzzles straight into two liters of maternity blood drenched in raw fifty-percent cresol and blistering chlorine gas!

*"Verdammte Scheiße, Feldwebel, der Hund kotzt sich die Lunge raus—alles voll Nachgeburt und Karbolgestank da unten, und Fleckfieber-Wäsche dampft im Kessel!"* coughed a handler's disgusted voice, scrambling backward up onto the glass blocks directly above Sylwia's shoulders.

Down in the eighty-eight-degree brick niche, knees aching on gritty stone, Sylwia hadn't paused for half a second.

Exteriorizing a bruised, crimson-purple loop of mid-ileum onto wet warm saline towels across Jacek's belly, she found the culprit immediately: the tumbling Mauser bullet had blown two ragged holes three centimeters apart right across the mesenteric border of the ileum, Thrombosing a wedge of mesenteric arcade and turning four inches of small bowel plum-black and dead, before lodging harmlessly inside the right internal oblique muscle wall.

No simple patch would hold; leave dead gut inside him and gangrene perforated tomorrow.

"Four non-crushing Doyen intestinal clamps—milk the bowel contents back six inches each way with your fingers, Halina, don't click steel ratchets loud," Sylwia breathed without moving her lips, communicating half by hand-sign in the amber gloom while boots still scraped undecidedly across the glass bricks four feet overhead.

Pinching the bowel lumen gently shut between Halina's steady fingers instead of ratcheting noisy metal teeth, Sylwia divided the necrotic four-inch segment cleanly with curved scissors, ligated three bleeding mesenteric vessels with fine silk, and laid the two healthy pink bowel ends squarely face-to-face for a two-layer hand-sewn end-to-end anastomosis.

Every stitch had to be watertight to the fraction of a millimeter: inner continuous locking Connell suture through all coats to invert mucosa cleanly without narrowing the lumen; outer ring of twenty-four interrupted fine silk Lembert seromuscular stitches three millimeters apart—*in, graze submucosa, out, square throw, cut*—her fingers flying in a silent, hypnotic blur learned across ten years and three thousand hours of trauma while sweat ran down between her shoulder blades under her smock and dripped off the tip of her chin onto sterile green toweling.

Overhead, the *Bahnschutz* sergeant spat loudly, barked *"Weiter zur Nowogrodzka—durch den Dreck kriecht kein Angeschossener ohne tot umzufallen!"* and marched his sneezing dogs and cursing patrol back out through the courtyard gates into the howling blizzard.

Neither woman breathed out until the distant truck engines ground into first gear and faded down Chałubińskiego.

Even then Sylwia didn't slow: scooping the deformed lead Mauser slug swiftly out of the right abdominal wall pocket with two fingers, lavaging the peritoneal cavity four times with warm boiled saline, dusting her last treasured five grams of German *Marfanil* sulfonamide powder straight over the sutured bowel ring before closing peritoneum and fascia with heavy figure-of-eight catgut and leaving the contaminated skin open around an iodoform wick.

Nineteen minutes from skin knife to final dressing.

Sliding two precious stolen ampoules of Słowik's *Cibazol* into Jacek's arm vein to fight peritonitis while typhus ran its twelve-day fever furnace, Sylwia stripped off her soaked rubber gloves and sank back onto her heels against a sack of soda ash, pushing a sweat-drenched strand of hair off her forehead with the back of her forearm.

Across the sleeping boy's blanketed knees, Halina Nowak caught Sylwia's bare, sublimate-reddened right hand without warning, bent her forehead straight down onto Sylwia's knuckles, and kissed them once—fiercely, wordlessly—before wiping her face dry and standing up to check the pulse.

"Don't thank me before Day Eight when typhus hits his myocardium, Halina," Sylwia murmured wearily, climbing to her stiff knees. "Nobody comes down this stair except you, Władek, and me—least of all Roman Karski."

Halina's dark eyes snapped sharp instantly at the tone around *Roman Karski*. She didn't ask why—in *Konspiracja* nurses didn't ask questions that cost lives—she simply nodded once like an axe falling: "If Karski touches that sluice latch, Pani Doktor, Władek puts a coal shovel through his ear."

---

# CHAPTER FOURTEEN

## Christmas in Ruins

**Warsaw — December 24, 1943**

When the first pale silver winter star pricked through gunmetal clouds above the ruined chimneys of Nowogrodzka at four-ten on Christmas Eve afternoon, Pavilion Three smelled of boiled red beets, dried mushrooms, carbolic acid, and pine needles.

Nobody owned an intact fir tree; old Władek had lopped four resinous green boughs off a cemetery spruce in Powązki and wired them above the scrubbed zinc instrument table in Minor Dressing, where Professor Tadeusz Majewski stood in a brushed black pre-war waistcoat holding a chipped porcelain saucer piled with paper-thin grey rectangles of unleavened *opłatek* wafer.

Eleven people stood in a quiet circle around the zinc table while blackout blinds shut out twenty-below frost: Majewski, Halina Nowak, two night sisters in starched wimples, one-armed fourteen-year-old Staś propped proudly in an oversize hospital bathrobe, Władek in clean collarless linen, three convalescent tramway workers, Dr. Roman Karski hanging ghost-pale near the radiator shadow, and Sylwia. Down in the brick niche behind the typhus boilers—where Halina would slip a warm cup of clear beet *barszcz* ten minutes from now—nineteen-year-old Jacek Nowak's typhus fever had broken cleanly at dawn on Day Ten, his sutured ileum passing gas and broth without a drop of leakage.

At the end of the table stood a single empty wooden chair draped with a clean white surgical towel before an untouched tin plate—the ancient Polish *Wigilia* place set for the wandering stranger, the imprisoned, and the dead who could not come home.

Every pair of eyes in the room touched that empty white chair and named its own ghosts in silence.

Majewski's thin, blue-veined hand trembled slightly as he broke off a corner of Sylwia's wafer and kissed both her lean cheeks. "That next Christmas finds Warsaw free, Pani Sylwia," the old professor murmured huskily, "and your hands still healing the living rather than sewing up the shot."

"And peace under your roof, Pan Profesor," Sylwia answered softly, breaking his wafer in turn before sharing hers with Halina—whose dark eyes shone luminous with unshed gratitude for the boy breathing quietly downstairs—and finally stepping across to Roman Karski by the radiator.

As Sylwia held out her grey wafer fragment toward him, Karski flinched microscopically before breaking off a tiny crumb with ice-cold, clammy fingers that avoided brushing hers. He couldn't meet her eyes; his gaze darted toward the empty white chair and back down to his shoes.

*"Zdrowia i spokoju, Roman,"* Sylwia wished him quietly—*Health and peace*—watching him swallow dryly as though unleavened flour choked his windpipe before he muttered an excuse about checking casualty admissions and slipped out into the dim corridor.

Forty-five minutes earlier, walking back from an emergency domiciliary call in Wola along the snow-choked lime avenue of Powązki Cemetery while early dusk turned ten thousand stone crosses blue-violet, Sylwia had heard dry snow crunch behind a granite mausoleum and turned to face Marek Zawadzki.

Carrying a wreath of frozen boxwood as cemetery cover, collar crusted white with rime from his own breath, Marek hadn't wasted ten seconds on holiday greetings.

"Rawicz convened sector security at noon today," Marek had told her, standing two paces off among snow-drifted graves while ravens cawed hoarsely in bare oaks overhead. "Twenty days have passed since we gave you those two ampoules on Żurawia, Sylwia. Schoeler isn't bedridden; our street spotters photographed him walking unassisted out of the Town Major's headquarters on Krakowskie Przedmieście yesterday morning without even a sling."

"I reported through Halina last week that Brandt seals every food tray and locks his medical cabinet," Sylwia replied calmly, cold air burning clean down her trachea. "And the Kampinos sweep map I pulled out of his study saved forty of *Grzyb*'s forest boys on December seventh, plus two Brest ammunition trains London bombed at Terespol."

"Which is the sole reason Rawicz hasn't convened a court-martial already," Marek snapped grimly, knocking snow off a marble plinth with his gloved fist. "Listen to me carefully—and don't interrupt, because what I am about to put in your ears carries your life on its back. Counter-Intelligence has taken Schoeler's liquidation out of your hands."

Inside Sylwia's wool gloves, every finger went cold as cemetery marble. Outwardly she didn't blink. "Poison failed, so he goes back to street shooting under Kutschera's hundred-for-one posters?"

"Special Kedyw six-man team—veterans of the Bürkl hit, three British Stens, two Gammon plastic charges, backup getaway car on Koszykowa," Marek said in flat, inexorable stone-cutter's Polish. "Monday morning, December twenty-seventh, at zero-eight-forty-five sharp. Every Monday and Thursday morning at eight-forty, Schoeler's Mercedes turns north off Aleja Róż onto Aleje Ujazdowskie and slows to second gear at the ulica Piękna corner where municipal snowbanks narrow the tramway."

Sylwia stared straight at the blue powder-burn scar on Marek's left temple—and saw the whole second trap yawn black beneath the snow before he even spoke the next sentence.

*Why on earth would an underground sector commander tell a suspected surgeon the exact street corner and minute of an upcoming Kedyw ambush?*

Marek's dark, tormented eyes met hers squarely across the frozen grave. He couldn't lie to her to save his soul.

"Nobody outside Rawicz, the assault lieutenant, and me knows Piękna at eight-forty-five," Marek said quietly, voice dropping raw with warning and grief. "Not the drivers until seven Monday morning; not Halina. Rawicz ordered me personally to brief you on the corner and hour this afternoon—ostensibly so you have emergency plasma ready at Lindleya if one of our boys takes a bullet pulling back along Koszykowa."

"And actually," Sylwia finished in a whisper colder than twenty below, "as a barium meal of Rawicz's own."

Marek didn't deny it. His jaw muscles bunched white beneath dark stubble. "If Schoeler cancels his Monday morning run to Krakowskie Przedmieście, switches streets to Marszałkowska, adds an armored scout car, or stays in bed with a convenient fever—Rawicz signs a *Wojskowy Sąd Specjalny* field execution warrant on you at nine-fifteen Monday morning, Sylwia, and sends two men I don't command to carry it out."

Stepping one stride closer between snow-buried iron grave-railings, Marek gripped her wool coat sleeve briefly—desperate, fierce, heartbreakingly loyal to the woman he thought he remembered from forty.

"Stay inside Pavilion Three from seven o'clock Monday morning, Sylwia," he whispered hoarsely. "Don't go near Aleja Róż after Sunday evening. Let six Stens finish what we missed on October nineteenth, and by Monday noon your slate with *Oddział II* is washed white forever. Swear to me you'll stay clear."

"I'll have plasma ready in Minor Dressing at eight-fifty," Sylwia had answered evenly, pulling free and walking down the long white avenue of graves without looking back while sixty-five hours began ticking steadily downward behind her ribs.

***

Two miles southeast at seven o'clock that same Christmas Eve, silver-tinsel swastikas glittered atop a twenty-foot Carpathian spruce inside the overheated crystal ballroom of the *Deutsches Haus* casino on Aleje Ujazdowskie, where eighty Wehrmacht, Luftwaffe, and civil administration officers sang *O Tannenbaum* over steamed carp and looted Reims Veuve Clicquot as loudly as men whistling past an open grave.

Standing near the tall frosted terrace doors holding an untouched glass of dry Sekt so hostesses wouldn't pester him, immaculate in field-grey with crimson collar tabs and the dented silver Humboldt medal riding silently inside his left breast pocket, Ludwig Schoeler watched Germany rot under electric candles.

Over by the buffet, fat SS-Doktor Krantz was drunkenly demonstrating tank pincers with breadsticks to two blonde Reich-post telephone girls; ten yards left, an artillery colonel freshly transferred off the Kiev salient stared blankly into his champagne glass with the grey, hollowed thousand-yard tremor of a man who had just watched sixty burned eighteen-year-old replacements frozen stiff into Ukrainian mud along the Zhitomir highway. Half the room knew the war in the East was irrevocably lost; a third drowned the knowledge in cognac; and the remaining sixth—men wearing black and silver runes near the punch bowl—grew deadlier by the week like scorpions trapped inside a ring of burning straw.

"Charming choir, isn't it, Herr Oberstleutnant?" murmured a mild, cultivated voice at Ludwig's left elbow.

Otto Reinhardt stood swirling two fingers of French Armagnac inside a balloon snifter, smelling faintly of yellow pear drops and pressed mouse-grey wool.

"Sturmbannführer," Ludwig inclined his head half an inch. "Quiet *Heiligabend* on Aleja Szucha?"

"Subversives respect church feasts surprisingly little," Reinhardt sighed amiably, eyes twinkling behind gold-wired lenses as he looked out at the snow-covered park trees. "Though Berlin gives us fresh administrative simplification for the New Year, I hear. Rumor along Prinz-Albrecht-Straße whispers that Admiral Canaris's *Abwehr* has suffered one embarrassment too many in Turkey and Madrid—Reichsführer Himmler may absorb military intelligence lock, stock, and cipher barrel before February. Imagine: all of us sitting under one tidy roof."

Beneath his field-grey wool, Ludwig kept every breath slow and bored, though the shaft went straight home—Canaris tottering in Berlin meant his own OKH shield could evaporate on forty-eight hours' teleprinter notice.

"Rumors warm Berlin clerks when coal runs short, Reinhardt," Ludwig drawled coolly, setting his untouched champagne flute onto a passing waiter's silver tray. "Wake me when the orders are printed. Frohe Weihnachten."

Walking briskly back through crisp, dry squeaking snow down Aleja Róż ten minutes later—boots ringing clear under iron streetlamps where sentries stamped numb feet beside striped sentry boxes—Ludwig let himself into Number Twelve and found Viktor Brandt waiting at the foot of the stairs, cheeks flushed cold bright pink from an errand near Dworzec Główny and eyes shining like a hunting terrier's.

"Christmas present from Ignacy Słowik's thirsty Baltic clerk on Szucha, Herr Oberstleutnant," Brandt murmured triumphantly as soon as they bolted the upstairs study door, drawing a folded photostatic sheet damp from fixer salts out of his leather map-case. "Cost us forty gold Reichsmarks in pre-war coin and three bottles of Martell—worth ten times the price. Original Referat Four internal liquidation receipt for *Sondertransport Hotel Polski — Liste B*, dated August nineteenth, nineteen-forty-three."

Ludwig carried the damp photostat directly under the green student lamp.

Typed in clean SS tabular columns ran seventy-four names of Polish Jews who had paid small fortunes for South American diplomatic papers at the Hotel Polski on ulica Długa in July—supposedly routed westward to Vittel internment camp in France for Allied civilian exchange. Column Four bore a single rubber-stamped notation opposite all seventy-four entries:

**SONDERBEHANDLUNG VOLLZOGEN — PAWIAK / RUINENGEBIET DZIELNA, 19.VIII.1943, 05.30 UHR.**
*Special treatment executed — Pawiak / Dzielna Ruins Area, August 19, 1943, 05:30 hours.*

Line Twenty-Nine read: **Goldblum, Estera — Geb. 14.V.1916 Warschau (Paraguay Nr. 419-B). Effekten verwertet.** *Personal effects salvaged.*

And scrawled neatly in green fountain-pen ink at the bottom under *Kenntnis genommen — Referat IV-A2*: **O. Reinhardt, SS-Sturmbannf.**

Ludwig stared down at the green-ink signature for five silent seconds. Even after Babi Yar briefings and Minsk transit reports, the tidy municipal-clerk neatness of *Effekten verwertet* beside a twenty-seven-year-old violinist's daughter whose dead handwriting Reinhardt was still forging onto Vittel blue note-cards four months later to buy cheap hospital espionage left an acrid, metallic taste at the back of his throat.

"Every blue note Reinhardt showed Doktor Karski since August was written over a lime pit," Brandt said quietly.

"Which means," Ludwig murmured, locking the damp photostat carefully inside his hidden wall-safe behind the landscape painting before checking his watch—quarter to eight, fifteen minutes before Sylwia's late *Wigilia* pass brought her across from Lindleya—"by tomorrow night or Sunday night at latest, Roman Karski belongs to us lock, stock, and syringe."

***

When Sylwia stepped inside the warm second-floor study at eight-fifteen, brushing dry powder-snow off her dark wool shoulders while Brandt tactfully retired to his ground-floor quarters with a bottle of Königsberg *Bärenfang* honey liqueur, Ludwig saw within half a second that something cold and heavy rode behind her grey eyes—deeper even than winter exhaustion.

Yet before he could ask, she glanced down at the mahogany table beside the roaring hearth and stopped dead.

Lying on a clean folded damask napkin beside two small crystal glasses of red Bordeaux sat two objects practically mythical in Warsaw of December nineteen-forty-three: a clean, rebound nineteen-twenty-eight first edition of Ferdinand Sauerbruch's *Die Chirurgie der Brustorgane*—the monumental two-volume Berlin bible of thoracic surgery whose plates she used to peer at hungrily through locked glass cases as a seventeen-year-old girl—and, resting right beside a bone-handled pocketknife, a single, glowing, pebble-skinned Sicilian blood-orange smelling impossibly of Mediterranean sun amidst twenty-below Polish frost.

Sylwia picked up the heavy leather spine of Sauerbruch gently with both bare hands as though touching warm bread in famine, opening instinctively to the chromolithograph plates of subclavian and transpleural approaches—and saw, penciled lightly on the flyleaf in razor-neat German script with no names signed in case Gestapo searches ever touched her shelves:

*1,00. — 24.XII.43.*

Her throat closed so tightly for three seconds she couldn't swallow.

"Where on earth did you steal Sauerbruch and an orange in Warsaw?" she whispered without looking up from the flyleaf.

"Sauerbruch from a bankrupt German book-dealer in Cracow via courier pouch; the orange off a Luftwaffe transport pilot flying lemons up from Reggio to Mauerwald mess," Ludwig answered quietly, stepping behind her to slide her damp charcoal coat off her shoulders and draw her back gently against his unbuttoned white shirtfront before the fire. Picking up the bone pocketknife, he sliced the small crimson-fleshed fruit cleanly down the equator into two fragrant halves, handing one half into her palm just as snow hissed against the hot chimney bricks overhead. "Still eat peel-pith and all when you're starving during study hour, Kotarska?"

Holding that fragrant red hemisphere of sunlight in her scarred surgeon's palm while sixty hours ticked inexorably toward Monday morning at eight-forty-five on the corner of Piękna Street—six Sten guns waiting behind snowbanks to tear this exact warm chest behind her spine into bloody shreds, and Major Rawicz's executioners waiting if Ludwig turned down Marszałkowska instead—Sylwia felt something fierce, cold, and incandescent crystallize cleanly inside her skull.

*Neither,* decided Dr. Sylwia Kotarska as clearly as clamping a bleeding vessel in pitch dark. *Rawicz doesn't get Ludwig's corpse, Reinhardt doesn't get our hospital, and Marek's six boys don't die in a German crossfire either.*

Turning smoothly inside the circle of Ludwig's arms to press a ruby segment of blood-orange straight between his parted lips—tart-sweet Sicilian juice bursting across both their tongues as he caught her wrist and kissed the juice cleanly off her mouth—Sylwia looked straight up into his slate-blue eyes when they parted for breath.

"Did Brandt get the Hotel Polski sheet?" she asked softly.

"Signed in Reinhardt's own green ink," Ludwig told her, dark satisfaction glinting in his gaze. "Estera Goldblum died at Pawiak on August nineteenth. Tomorrow night—or Sunday night after ward rounds—we corner Roman Karski alone in Pavilion Three's pathology basement and break Reinhardt's leash clean in half."

"Sunday evening at seven," Sylwia said instantly, brain racing six moves ahead across every street intersection between Lindleya, Aleja Szucha, and Piękna like a master chess player seeing a forced mate through smoke. "Not tomorrow—tomorrow is Christmas Day and half the ward sisters stay late for vespers. Sunday night at seven the pathology wing is dead empty, and Karski has night admission duty."

She rested both bare palms flat against Ludwig's warm chest—right over the healed pink scar beneath his left collarbone and the firm callus bridging the bone—looking up at him in the quiet Christmas firelight with eyes fierce as winter stars.

"Bring that August nineteenth execution sheet to the pathology cellar door at seven Sunday night, Ludwig," she whispered, rising on tiptoe to kiss the crescent scar above his right eyebrow before pulling his mouth down to hers. "Because by eight-thirty Monday morning, Roman Karski is going to hand Otto Reinhardt the biggest Christmas package Aleja Szucha ever unwrapped."

***

Later that Christmas Eve, after midnight bells had died away across frozen parks and the anthracite in the iron grate had burned down to deep, smokeless ruby caverns throwing rose-gold shadow across the ceiling moldings, neither of them slept.

Sylwia lay naked on her side beneath the heavy goose-down coverlet facing the hearth, Ludwig stretched behind her with one lean thigh hooked warm between both of hers and his left forearm resting lightly along the curve of her hip—healed solid enough now that five weeks of careful immobilization had turned shattered bone into living bridge. Outside on the mahogany table, peeled crimson rinds of the Sicilian blood-orange perfumed the cold room air with an impossible ghost of Palermo orchards and pre-war Mediterranean summers neither of them had ever seen together.

With slow, almost clinical absorption—because somewhere down behind her ribs ticked fifty-six silent hours until six Kedyw Sten guns waited in snowbanks along Piękna Street, and every surgeon knew even the cleanest operation carried five percent mortal hazard when grease froze or sentries panicked—Sylwia lifted Ludwig's bare right hand into the dim red firelight and traced every bone from radial styloid to fingertip with her thumb.

Callus on the outer side of the middle phalanx from seventeen years of pens and compass dividers; faded white sabre-fencing nick across the first knuckle from Königsberg; faint dark powder speckle near the thumb web from Orel.

"You're cataloguing bones again, *Frau Doktor*," Ludwig murmured into the loose dark hair behind her ear, voice low and lazy with rare midnight peace, kissing the sensitive hollow at the nape of her neck where fine curls met warm skin. "Planning an amputation?"

"Checking circulation," Sylwia lied softly, pressing his open palm flat down between her bare breasts so he felt the steady beat underneath before turning half around inside his arms to look at his face in the red coal-glow. Up close without uniform wool or Generalstab steel in his spine, thirty-two looked startlingly close to the eighteen-year-old boy who had sketched her hand by candle-lantern light high up in the Berlin State Library dome fourteen Decembers ago—save for the deep bracket war had chiseled beside his mouth and the quiet shadow behind his eyes whenever Konrad's name came near. "Is your mother still in the Dahlem house tonight?"

Ludwig's fingers stilled slightly over her ribs before resuming a slow, gentle stroke along her bare collarbone.

"No," he said quietly. "RAF Lancasters dropped two four-thousand-pound blockbusters onto Thielplatz in late August—blew the roof and conservatory glass clean into the garden pond. She moved east to my aunt's timber manor near Allenstein in Masuria. Writes me three formal lines once a fortnight on thick cream paper: *Frost arrived Tuesday; Pastor Ehlers sends regards; see that you wear flannel beneath your tunic.* Not one word about Konrad since July, and not one word about Hitler—because she knows censor clerks steam open Generalstab mail."

Sylwia studied the pale gleam of his Baltic eyes in the embers. "If she knew whose head was lying on your pillow tonight in Warsaw?"

A dry, genuine ghost of a laugh vibrated silently through Ludwig's bare chest against hers.

"My father would spin fast enough inside Invalidenfriedhof granite to power half Berlin's trams," Ludwig murmured, brushing a dark strand back behind her ear with infinite, lingering tenderness. "My mother? Margarethe Schoeler comes from Hamburg merchant bankers who counted gold marks long before Hohenzollerns wore crowns. After five minutes' icy silence over her teacup, she would inspect your posture, note your French accent beats hers, decide anybody tough enough to pull her surviving son back from dead shock in forty minutes flat had acceptable breeding after all, and complain your hands smelled of carbolic."

Despite Powązki's graves and Rawicz's gun waiting at Monday noon, a quiet, involuntary smile curved Sylwia's mouth in the dark—sharp and aching because imagining an ordinary teacup in a house without machine guns felt more indecent than nakedness.

Rolling her gently flat onto her back beneath the down quilt so red hearth-light washed warm across her bare throat and shoulders, Ludwig propped himself above her on his healthy right elbow, tracing one slow fingertip from the hollow between her collarbones straight down between her breasts to her navel—watching gooseflesh rise instantly in his wake and her breath hitch shallow and hot.

"When you walked down those terrace steps at Wannsee in red silk fourteen summers ago," Ludwig whispered, dropping his mouth to kiss the exact path his finger had just burned down her sternum, "do you know what two lines of Goethe ran straight through my skull while Bredow spilled Moselle down his cuff?"

Her hands slid into his cropped tarnished-gold hair, pulling him closer as his warm knee nudged her thighs wider beneath the quilt. "Tell me."

Against the bare, beating skin below her left breast, just before he slid slowly, hot and heavy and familiar, deep inside the waiting wet heat of her body—stealing her breath clean away in a silent, arching gasp under the warm feathers—Ludwig whispered two quiet German lines from the *West-östlicher Divan* straight onto her skin:

*"Gottes ist der Orient, Gottes ist der Okzident—"*
*"Und in deinen Augen allein liegt mein Vaterland."*

*East and West belong to God—and in your eyes alone lies my country.*

Sylwia pulled his mouth fiercely up to hers, wrapping both bare legs tight around his lean hips in the dark red glow, holding him deep inside her with every ounce of strength in her body while Christmas snow whispered against blacked-out glass and Monday morning waited out in the frost.

---

# CHAPTER FIFTEEN

## Hotel Polski

**Warsaw — December 26–27, 1943**

Down in the subterranean pathology wing beneath Infant Jesus Hospital's north courtyard, twenty-below December frost seeped straight through three-foot granite foundation walls and coated the white floor tiles in glistening hoarfrost.

Four zinc-lined mortuary drawers stood latched along the east wall; down the center of the low-vaulted dissecting room ran two grooved Carrara marble autopsy tables smelling permanently of forty-percent formalin, carbolic acid, and cold tallow. Above Table One burned a single green-shaded hundred-watt porcelain lamp, casting stark chalk shadows across rows of stoppered glass specimen jars where cirrhotic livers and tuberculous lungs floated grey and timeless in yellow fluid.

At five minutes past seven on Sunday evening—Saint Stephen's night, when snow drove horizontally across empty Warsaw courtyards and only skeleton crews staffed the upper pavilions—Dr. Roman Karski pushed open the heavy zinc-sheathed swing door at the foot of the basement stairs, shivering inside his unbuttoned white doctor's coat.

"Pani Doktor?" Karski called nervously, squinting past the hanging lamp. Five minutes ago night porter Władek had knocked on Admissions to say Frau Doktor Kotarska needed a second physician's counter-signature on an unclaimed street-exposure corpse before the municipal wagon arrived. Yet both marble slabs lay bare and dry. "Władek said you had a frostbite mortality down—"

Behind his back, the outer iron corridor grille clicked shut softly, and Hauptmann Viktor Brandt slipped a heavy brass padlock through the staple from the stairwell side.

Karski spun violently around—pinprick pupils widening in instant animal panic—and saw two people standing motionless inside the room.

Beside the specimen shelves stood Sylwia in her dark wool coat, hands empty at her sides. And stepping slowly out of the shadowed archway behind Table Two, tall in an unmarked dark-charcoal civilian winter loden coat worn open over crimson-striped General Staff riding breeches, stood Oberstleutnant Ludwig Schoeler.

For two heartbeats Karski's breath rattled dry through parted yellowing teeth. No trapped creature needed explanations; one look at Sylwia Kotarska standing unguarded beside the German colonel he had been reporting on to Otto Reinhardt for eight weeks told Roman Karski his tightrope had snapped over the pit.

His right hand plunged inside his white coat pocket and jerked out a blued-steel 6.35-millimeter pocket Sauer automatic—bought off a drunken Luftwaffe corporal in forty-one for suicide if Pawiak ever knocked, safety snapped off with a frantic *click* as the short barrel wavered wildly between Sylwia's chest and Ludwig's throat.

"Stay back—don't move a step, either of you!" Karski's voice cracked half an octave high, echoing thinly off cold tiles while morphine-starved sweat beaded shiny across his greenish forehead. "I knew it—Reinhardt suspected it from the first week! A Polish surgeon whoring for *Abwehr* behind locked doors! One pull of this trigger and I walk straight out to the SD post on Nowogrodzka and tell Sturmbannführer Reinhardt I caught you two conspiring against him!"

Neither Sylwia nor Ludwig raised a hand or backed half an inch.

Standing six feet across the grooved marble dissecting slab from the trembling pistol muzzle, Ludwig didn't even glance toward the gun. Reaching calmly inside his dark loden breast pocket with his right hand, he drew out two folded photostatic documents and slid them smoothly across slick white Carrara marble until they came to rest directly under the hundred-watt lamp two feet from Karski's elbow.

"Before you run to Nowogrodzka to collect your next five ampoules of Bayer morphine, *Jaskółka*," Ludwig said in quiet, level German devoid of threat or heat, "read Line Twenty-Nine under the lamp."

At his code name spoken aloud out of Ludwig Schoeler's mouth, Karski jerked as though struck across the face with wet wire. "I don't know what—"

"Read the sheet, Roman," Sylwia said softly in Polish. Not angry; horribly, unbearably tired. "We already photographed the quinine-anthracene ultraviolet dye on your right thumb from the courtyard payphone and the pharmacy safe two weeks ago. If we wanted you shot in an alley by Kedyw, you wouldn't have lived to eat Christmas Eve wafer on Friday. Look at the marble."

Hypnotized against his will by that terrible quietness in her voice, keeping the little Sauer wavering chest-high in his right fist, Karski edged one shuffling step sideways to the head of the autopsy slab and looked down under the glare of the porcelain shade.

Top sheet first: his own enlarged right thumbprint photographed beside a calibration ruler, glowing bright spectral white from fluorescent quinine crystals transferred off Schedule Alpha.

Beneath it lay the second photostat.

Eagle-and-swastika letterhead of *Der Kommandeur der Sicherheitspolizei und des SD für den Distrikt Warschau — Referat IV-A2*. Date stamp: **19. August 1943**. Heading: **Vollzugsmeldung — Sondertransport Hotel Polski (Liste B, Südamerika-Pässe)**.

Karski's free left hand gripped the cold raised blood-groove rim of the marble slab. His lips moved soundlessly over German administrative columns he understood all too fluently after four years of occupation decrees. Down past *Goldberg... Goldfarb...*

**29. Goldblum, Estera — Geb. 14.V.1916 Warschau (Paraguay-Pass Nr. 419-B). Sonderbehandlung vollzogen — Pawiak / Ruinengebiet Dzielna, 19.VIII.1943, 05.30 Uhr.**

*Effekten verwertet: 1 Damenmantel (Wolle), 1 Paar Schuhe, 1 Goldmedaillon (Geigenform, 14 Karat, 11,2 g).*

*One gold locket, violin-shaped, fourteen karat, eleven point two grams.*

And at the foot of the column, signed cleanly in flowing fountain-pen script: **O. Reinhardt, SS-Sturmbannf.**

Silence hummed inside the freezing pathology vault like high-tension current. Somewhere behind the brick wall, subterranean water dripped slowly into a floor drain—*plink... plink... plink*.

Karski let out a strange, rusty laugh that sounded like torn tin scraping stone, shaking his head rapidly from side to side while spittle wet his chin.

"Cheap *Abwehr* forgery," Karski whispered wildly, thrusting the little automatic two inches closer toward Ludwig's chest as though lead could shoot holes through August nineteenth. "Do you think I was born yesterday, Herr Oberstleutnant?! Typewriters take five minutes to fake! I saw Estera's own handwriting eight days ago at Dworzec Gdański—blue French stationery from Vittel camp in the Vosges! *Najdroższy Romku, dają mleko i czekamy na wymianę!* Nobody on earth knew she called me *Romku* with that curl on the *R* except me!"

"Nobody," Sylwia said very gently from six feet away, "except whoever read the three old letters sitting inside that gold violin-shaped locket when they stripped her coat off at Pawiak on August nineteenth, Roman. Did you ever tell Otto Reinhardt what shape locket you bought her on Nowy Świat when she passed her conservatory jury in thirty-eight?"

Karski's mouth stayed half open. No sound came out.

"And think back to that blue card Reinhardt flashed for two seconds before pulling it out of your fingers," Sylwia continued quietly, ruthlessly, cutting dead gangrene down to living bone because mercy lied and anatomy never did. "You showed me Estera's concert invitation once before the war. Educated in Lwów, wasn't she? Lwów girls cross the Polish *ł* right across the top tip of the stem like a T. How was *dają mleko* crossed on Reinhardt's blue paper?"

Behind Karski's bloodshot eyes, memory developed like photographic plate dropped into acid: cramped grey pencil lead on pale blue paper across a mahogany pharmacy table, *dają mleko*, the diagonal slash cutting straight through the middle belly of the *ł* in neat Warsaw clerk style—and Reinhardt sliding the card swiftly back inside calfskin before Karski's wet thumb could touch fresh graphite.

*Eleven point two grams. Violin-shaped.*

He had never once mentioned a violin locket to Otto Reinhardt.

Slowly, as if every tendon behind his knees had been severed simultaneously with a razor, Roman Karski slid downward along the white-tiled pedestal of Autopsy Table One until both knees hit frosted porcelain with a dull *thud*.

The cocked 6.35-millimeter Sauer dropped out of slackening fingers—bouncing once without discharging—and skidded across the tiles to stop against the toe of Ludwig's riding boot. Ludwig stooped unhurriedly, clicked the safety catch back on, and slipped the tiny pistol into his loden coat pocket.

Doubled completely in half with his forehead pressed flat against the freezing blood-gutter of the marble dissecting slab, hands clawing into his own thinning hair, Roman Karski made a low, protracted, sub-human howling sound deep behind clenched teeth—four months of filthy betrayal, selling Tomek's life, selling hospital nights, groveling in cinders for twenty-milligram vials of poppy juice while Estera's bones had lain dissolved under quicklime in the Dzielna rubble since high summer.

Sylwia stood watching him without moving. Part of her remembered Tomek's four torn nail-beds down in Cell Four and felt zero pity; the surgeon half looked down at a man whose soul Otto Reinhardt had vivisected alive without anesthetic.

After ninety seconds the dry retching subsided. Karski lifted a face aged twenty years—chalk-grey, smeared with tears and frost-condensation off the marble—and looked blindly up at Sylwia.

"Give me back that pistol, Sylwia," Karski whispered in Polish. "One round in the mouth over the drain—Władek washes the tiles in five minutes and nobody wastes rope on me."

"Killing yourself on my floor tonight gives Otto Reinhardt clean warning by nine tomorrow morning and buys Estera nothing," Sylwia answered coldly, kneeling on one knee three feet away from him so her storm-grey eyes locked straight into his shattered gaze. "Do you want to die clean like a dog hiding under stairs, Roman, or do you want to drive four inches of broken glass down Otto Reinhardt's throat before you go?"
